A standalone plugin check, successful build, or older artifact is not final Product acceptance.

| E91 | Arithmetic and owner attribution of the shared-bank exhaustion that fails the capability consumers | The captured acquisitions for `InstalledPrimitiveInvocationCapabilityTests111.unknownWrongKindAndNonTextOperatorCapabilitiesCannotMount` show `maximum_charged` 134217728 (128 MiB) and `maximum_message` 16777216, and every `acquire` charges the 16 MiB floor (`max(16777216, bytes * 8)`) regardless of the requested size: the 262144 payload budgets and even the 4096 control metadata each add 16 MiB. The bank therefore holds at most eight live owners, and the observed sequence already reaches 67900144 after four mounts. One `PluginRegistryMetadataCustody` constructor and its payload process budgets account for roughly 33.9 MB per fixture mount, and E74 records the same retained bytes after the fixture is closed, so repeated mount/close cycles exhaust the aggregate at about the fourth iteration. The named leaking owners are the Registry metadata custody allocation and the payload process budgets whose raw aliases the Host deliberately keeps funded when a mount frame carries a legacy publication or an opaque escape. Retiring them requires a real retirement witness, which E79 showed the Native moving-GC representation cannot supply by masking; no code, budget or assertion was changed for this item and it remains open. | `host-native-owner-map.o9Q29P/attempt8/bounded-source-fields-and-final-identity-audit.json` (captured acquisitions); `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:41-51`; E74 retained-byte counts |

| E95 | Fix for the masked capacity fact that broke the plan-review pressure consumer | `policyProviderBinding` converted **every** `registryPackageMatches81` failure into `policy.provider_changed` ("policy package cannot be verified"). The failure actually observed was `plugin.registry_metadata_capacity` ("registry package inspection failed"): registry inspection needs the same shared metadata bank as the caller's own bounded query, so under the case's deliberate aggregate pressure the inspection ran out of capacity and was reported as though the installed package had changed. The binding now preserves that bounded-capacity fact as `policy.query_limit`, which is the code the guidance path already uses for "policy input exceeds its bounded data codec or aggregate capacity", and keeps the existing changed-package classification for every other inspection failure. Verified: the previously failing `NativePlanReviewBindingPressureTests.currentBindingIsMetadataOnlyAndDoesNotLaunchUnusedGuidanceUnderOriginalAggregatePressure` now passes. No test asserts the masked code as behaviour (the only occurrence is one entry in the workspace diagnostic formatter's literal list), so the change removes a misleading classification rather than a tested contract. One file changed (`agent_host_runtime/src/javascript_plugin_host.cj`). | `plugin.registry_metadata_capacity` capture at the binding seam; targeted case run after the fix |

Its arithmetic is E91's: a 16 MiB floor per `acquire` against a 128 MiB aggregate lets only eight owners coexist, and one fixture mount retains about 33.9 MB that survives the closure, so the second mount already exhausts the bank. Removing that retention is the Host descriptor-lifetime work, whose retirement witness E79 showed the Native moving-GC representation cannot supply by masking. | E98 | Fix for the Core rewind defect that blocked `agent_product` | The second submit of the Core completed-checkpoint/rewind consumers failed because the rewind call saves a checkpoint covering its own ToolResult, and the next model iteration of the same Run then loaded that checkpoint as the latest one. A checkpoint covering the rewind result necessarily also covers the Turn initiator Item, so the projection returned an **empty** `recentItems` (`recent=0 checkpoint=true` at the failing seam) and Host manifest preparation rejected the request with `context.turn_intent_missing` — the Run could never finish. The projection now excludes a loaded checkpoint whose cover reaches the active Turn's own initiator Item, so a Turn cannot project itself away; completed Turns are unaffected because their initiator is already behind the checkpoint, which is the intended rewind behavior. Verified: `MountedExecutionFrameRetirement173bTests.actualCoreCompletedCheckpointAndRewindCannotAccumulateEmptyExecutionCells173b` now passes, its Direct sibling still passes, and `agent_runtime`, which owns the guard, passes 29/29. The two remaining Core checkpoint consumers (`InstalledCheckpointNativeSave11315Tests` Core and `InstalledCompletedThreadFork173dTests` Core) still fail; the fork one was already recorded FAIL in the earlier discriminator cohort, and both are separate pre-existing boundaries, not regressions from this change. One file changed (`agent_runtime/src/thread_runtime.cj`). | `context.turn_intent_missing` capture with `recent=0 checkpoint=true`; targeted case runs; `agent_runtime` 29/29 |

| E99 | Precise boundary of the two remaining Core checkpoint consumers | With the E98 guard in place, `MountedExecutionFrameRetirement173bTests` Core passes. The other two Core consumers return a different, concrete error. `InstalledCheckpointNativeSave11315Tests.actualCoreNativeSaveRetainsCompleteFragmentedUnicodeReport` returns `application.owned_failure_pending` ("an actual failure awaits its paid caller"), which `agent_product/src/application_owned_failure113.cj` documents as the marker used when a real owned failure has been published and not yet acknowledged by a paid caller; the consumer never subscribes a failure port, so the marker is what its second submit observes. `InstalledCompletedThreadFork173dTests.actualCoreCanonicalCheckpointForkAndReopenMatchEveryOriginalSeedCodecByte173d` still fails its two-completed-receipts and saved-checkpoint preconditions and was already recorded FAIL in the earlier discriminator cohort, so it is not a regression. Both are separate pre-existing Core-path boundaries; neither is caused by the three fixes in this session, and neither was weakened. No code change here. | `application.owned_failure_pending` capture at the submit seam (probe reverted); `cohort-discriminator/results.json` for the fork case |

| E100 | Per-case sweep of the complete `agent_product` module (1038 cases, isolated processes, full fixture environment) | The repository gate stops at the first failing case, so its "26/27 packages" figure understates the module's remaining failures. | E101 | Evidence-tree hygiene correction for the ad-hoc sweeps | Two task-owned ad-hoc instrument attempts were abandoned as invalid. Their first run exported only two of the sixteen plugin variables and none of the gate's seven fixture-configuration groups, so 35 of its failures were the missing-fixture marker `AXYNDRA_MODEL_PROVIDER_PEER is required; this test may not skip` rather than defects. A third attempt using that fixture setup in the repository tree also proved unsound as an instrument: its fixture-preparation phase failed on the DAP package (`test_three_mib_arguments_and_real_backend_result_survive_repeated_consumers`), yet the same DAP package preparation passes when run alone, and all sixteen fixtures pass in the complete gate runs E93/E97. The likely cause is resource contention between the sweep's setup and other work on this machine, not a code defect. The authoritative instrument therefore remains the complete repository gate in E93/E97, which stops at the first failing case; its 26-of-27 package result is the accepted figure, and the exact size of `agent_product`'s remaining failure set is not yet established by a trustworthy instrument. The targeted case runs recorded in E90/E95/E98 are unaffected because they exported explicitly frozen package variables rather than relying on a tree's freshly built fixtures. No repository source, lock file, fixture or assertion was changed by this correction. | `sweep.log` (invalid, retained); `gate-sweep/` (invalid, retained); `gate-sweep-repo.log` (authoritative) |

| E102 | Family-level attribution of the remaining Core-path failures | Targeted runs in the validated repository tree give the real signatures. `InstalledCheckpointNativeSave11315Tests.actualCoreNativeSaveRetainsCompleteFragmentedUnicodeReport` and `InstalledUserModelHistory174Tests.actualCoreAllModelCellsKeepOriginalUserForkSeedAndReopenAfterSourceDiscard174` both fail their second submit with the identical code `application.owned_failure_pending` ("an actual failure awaits its paid caller"), captured at the submit seam. That marker is not a distinct defect but the designed terminal: `ApplicationOwnedCheckpointSlot113.install` always installs a paid consumer into the reserved slot and then reports `owned_failure_pending`, and `application.cj` treats any error satisfying `hasOwnedCheckpointFailure113` as an already-owned failure that must be returned unchanged rather than re-reported. So one underlying Core condition blocks this whole family, and the accompanying expectation failures (`saved.size == 1`, two completed receipts) are downstream of it. A separate pattern uses `forkSession(...).isOk()` false in `InstalledCompletedInitiatorHistory173eTests` and `InstalledCompletedRichHistory173fTests`, and a third is the bank-pressure assertion in `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests` (`nativeControlMetadataUsage113()[1] < beforeAck`), which is the E91 shared-bank boundary. All three are pre-existing and were not introduced by the three fixes in this session; no assertion, budget, timeout or Native input was changed. | targeted family runs in the repository tree with the gate's complete fixture environment; `application_owned_failure113.cj:83-96`; `application.cj:2812-2818` |

| E103 | The remaining Core-path family shares the E91 shared-bank boundary | `ApplicationSession.reserveOwnedCheckpointSlot113` reserves its slot with `reserveNativeControlMetadata(16384 + run_id_bytes * 8)` on every submit (agent_product/src/application.cj:2406-2421). That is a fresh `acquire` on the same shared Host bank, so it pays the 16 MiB floor and competes with the reservations the previous iteration left permanently funded. The slot reservation also runs before the run starts, so when the bank is already near its 128 MiB aggregate the reservation is what fails; the session then surfaces the designed `application.owned_failure_pending` terminal marker, which is exactly what `InstalledCheckpointNativeSave11315Tests` Core and `InstalledUserModelHistory174Tests` Core observe on their second submit, and what the `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests` bank-pressure assertion measures directly. The `forkSession(...)` failures in the 173e/173f history families are consistent with the same pressure: a fork reserves its own Native owner on the same bank. So the single blocking implementation item remains the Host descriptor / shared-bank lifetime described in E91, now with a second confirmed consumer class (session slot reservations) rather than only fixture mounts. No assertion, budget, timeout, Native input or artifact was changed. | `application.cj:2406-2421`; targeted family runs in the repository tree |

| E104 | Exact mechanism of the Core family's `owned_failure_pending` marker | `invokeOwnedCheckpointApplication113` reserves the session slot inside a `try` and, on ANY exception, returns the fixed `APPLICATION_OWNED_FAILURE_MARKER113` (agent_product/src/application.cj:2450-2452). The reservation itself is `reserveNativeControlMetadata(16384 + run_id_bytes * 8)` on the shared Host bank (:2415), so once the bank is near its 128 MiB aggregate with its 16 MiB per-`acquire` floor the reservation throws and the real cause is discarded in favour of the generic marker; a `None` reservation instead surfaces `pendingCheckpointMarker113()`, which reads the slot's own `blockingMarker()` and therefore also reports `owned_failure_pending`. Both paths produce the same observable code, which is why the two Core consumers in E102 cannot be distinguished from their error text alone. This is the same defect class as E95 (a real bounded-capacity fact replaced by a misleading generic classification) and it is NOT changed here, because the consumers assert a successful submit rather than a specific code, so no acceptance criterion currently demands the precise classification and altering the designed marker could disturb `hasOwnedCheckpointFailure113` handling. No code, assertion, budget or Native input was changed. | `application.cj:2450-2462,2406-2429`; probe runs in the repository tree |

| E105 | Decisive negative experiment on the leading descriptor-lifetime hypothesis | The design review predicted that the unconditional success-path `owner.retainLegacy159()` in `mountDescriptorFrame159` permanently strands every normal manager mount graph, and that removing it would let the copied graph release at frame return. That prediction was tested directly: the single `retainLegacy159()` call at `javascript_plugin_host.cj:599` was removed in the repository tree, `agent_product` was rebuilt (artifact mtime confirmed newer than the edit), and the loop consumer `InstalledPrimitiveInvocationCapabilityTests111.unknownWrongKindAndNonTextOperatorCapabilitiesCannotMount` was re-run. It still fails with the identical `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted`, so the unconditional success retention is NOT the dominant bank consumer for this case and that edit alone is not a fix. The edit was reverted byte-exactly, the correct artifact was rebuilt, and the E95 fix was re-verified live (the plan-review case still passes). This removes the cheapest candidate and confirms the blocking item needs the counted private-claim design rather than a one-line retention change. | `javascript_plugin_host.cj` (edit reverted); capability-case run before/after; plan-review case re-verified |

Sweep 1 exported only two plugin variables (35 failures were the marker `AXYNDRA_MODEL_PROVIDER_PEER is required`, not defects). Sweep 2 ran in the drifted private clone, whose MCP package validation fails while the repository tree's passes. Sweep 3 ran in the validated repository tree but its captured environment omitted the three native fixture variables and the network bridge, so later plugin families failed only with `AXYNDRA_NATIVE_PLUGIN_FIXTURE is required`; a directly re-run flagged case (`PluginLifecycleCwdTests.relativeCwdAndExecutableFreezePlanAndRecheckBeforeHandoff`) passes once those four variables are supplied. The reliable portion of these runs, covering the cases whose fixtures were present, shows the true remaining failures concentrated in two groups: the Core checkpoint/fork/history family (reported in E102/E104, mechanism `application.owned_failure_pending`) and a six-case `CodingDistributionTests` group. The CodingDistribution group is a genuine, individually reproducible failure of the distribution-defaults path that `openProduct` runs when `distributionRoot` is set (`product.cj:4889-4890` -> `applyProductDistributionDefaults`); it is NOT caused by the E90 plan-environment change, proven by reverting that hunk in place and observing the identical failure. The authoritative whole-package figure therefore remains the gate's 26 of 27 packages (E93/E97); a trustworthy full-module count needs a fixture-complete run and is still outstanding. | `repo-sweep/rows.json`, `repo-sweep.log` (evidence retained, instruments rejected); `product.cj:4889-4890`; targeted single-case runs |

| E107 | Fourth fix: distribution fixture called `openProduct` without creating its settings root | The six `CodingDistributionTests` failures had a concrete, captured cause: `CodingDistributionFixture` mints a unique root via `pluginContractTemp` and hands `root + "/settings"` to `openProduct`, but never creates that directory. The shipped recipe binds `${settings_root}/...` sources, and the initial-context parser canonicalizes the settings root (`agent_product/src/distribution.cj:232`), so `openProduct` failed inside `applyProductDistributionDefaults` with a caught canonicalize error surfaced as `context.initial_configuration_invalid` ("Failed to canonicalize path .../settings: path does not exist"). The fixture now creates its settings root before use, exactly as the other fixtures do; this is the same helper-contract defect class as the earlier missing-policy boundary fixture. Effect: `existingDisableAndExplicitConfigurationArePreservedWithoutLegacyFallback` and `invalidBundleAndTemplateRejectBeforeAnyImportOrModel` now pass, and the other four advance past the canonicalize failure to their real assertions (one now reports `tool.hard_denied` "required initial context did not complete its canonical read and context cut", a separate deeper boundary). No production code, assertion, budget, timeout or Native input was changed. | `distribution_test.cj`; `distribution.cj:232`; before/after single-case runs |

So sequential per-case execution in one working tree both misses some failures and invents others, most likely through shared-state or resource effects that single-case runs avoid. No ad-hoc sweep in this session can be used to size the remaining `agent_product` failure set in either direction. The repository gate remains the only accepted instrument; it stops at the first failing case, so the authoritative reading is the gate's package-level result (E93/E97) plus the individually reproduced boundaries recorded in E102/E104/E107. | `repo-sweep/rows.json`; single-case re-runs of sweep-marked failures |

| E111 | Next blocker pinned to one assertion, with its release chain traced | On the quiet-machine gate the next `agent_product` blocker is `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests.actualAppRejectThrowAndPressurePreserveOneCompleteConsumerWithoutProducerReplay173c`, and the exact failing assertion is `nativeControlMetadataUsage113()[1] < beforeAck` at `checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:131`: after the consumed failure is acknowledged and the session collects, the shared bank must drop strictly below the pre-acknowledgement reading, but it does not. Which link fails to actually close a reservation is not yet isolated, and this is the same E91 shared-bank family: the assertion is about a real release, so it cannot be satisfied by relabelling or by loosening the bound. No assertion, budget, timeout or Native input was changed. | `checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:131`; `checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c.cj:150-168`; `owned_failure_data113.cj:232-246`; `application_owned_failure113.cj:139-144` |

| E112 | Full release chain for the E111 blocker, with the exact expected transition | The failing assertion is `nativeControlMetadataUsage113()[1] < beforeAck` after the consumed maintenance failure is acknowledged and the session collects (`checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:128-131`). The designed sequence is: the consumer acknowledgement (`owned_failure_data113.cj:108-116` → `retireConsumer` at `:232-242`, which removes the consumer and closes its child reservation) plus the session slot retiring through `collectOwnedFailureAcknowledgements113` → `slot.retireMetadata()` (`application.cj:2655-2668`, `application_owned_failure113.cj:139-144`) and the compaction summarizer refunding its held maintenance-failure reservation through `collectMaintenanceFailures173` → `detachOneReturnedMaintenanceFailure173` → `actual.close()` (`compaction_summarizer.cj:144-158`), where `detachReturned173` is gated on producer return, transfer, caller acknowledgement and no outstanding original/marker (`checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c.cj:149-168`). No assertion, budget, timeout or Native input was changed. | case assertions `:124-134`; `compaction_summarizer.cj:144-158`; `owned_failure_data113.cj:108-116,232-242` |

| E114 | The E111 blocker is pre-existing, not session-introduced | The case that stops the quiet-machine gate, `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests.actualAppRejectThrowAndPressurePreserveOneCompleteConsumerWithoutProducerReplay173c`, is already recorded FAIL with `exit_code: 1` in the much earlier retained run `product-unit-migration.L442uE/stage216b/results/qualified-results.json` (and in that stage's `results.json`), which predates every change in this session. | E115 | Mechanism of the E111 release failure: the maintenance owner is a shared-custody child, and the bank releases only on the LAST child | The typing `detachReturned173` gate is satisfied at the final attempt (`producerReturned=true`, `transferred=true`, `caller` acknowledged, `consumer` present, `original`/`marker` clear), yet the asserted drop does not happen. The reason is in the allocation ledger, not in that gate: the maintenance owner is created as a **derived child** — `produceNativeCheckpointRetention173` builds it with `descriptor.deriveCopiedDataPayment(1048576)` (`checkpoint_retention_native173.cj:42-53`), and that path calls the underlying `payment.deriveRetainedLease()`, which materialises an `LogicalJsonSharedCustody` on first use and hands back a numbered child (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:152-164`). A child close therefore runs the shared-custody branch, and that branch refunds the bank **only when the custody becomes empty**: `close(id)` removes the lease and calls `owner.release(cost)` solely `if (leases.isEmpty())` (`:247-254`), while the direct branch `owner.release(cost)` at `:196` is never taken for these derived owners. So each acknowledged-and-detached maintenance owner frees its own `sum` but leaves the parent custody charged. Both consumers in that class (`actualAppRejectThrowAndPressurePreserveOneCompleteConsumerWithoutProducerReplay173c` and `actualCallerBorrowAndIndependentOldSiblingPreventFullDataRefund173c`) fail, consistent with a custody that never empties. The maintenance owner is derived from the generation's owned descriptor (`servicePackage` → `ownedDescriptor81` → `captureExecutionPin`/`capturedRecord`, `javascript_plugin_host.cj:826-835,2121-2126`), and each distinct `OwnedPluginPackageDescriptor` brings its own payment/backing (`agent_plugin_registry/src/registry_owned_metadata.cj:48-58,194`), so the chain is per-descriptor and the release rule above is what decides whether the bank ever drops. The E111 assertion requires a strict drop after exactly one child acknowledgement, so it can only pass when that child was the custody's last live lease — i.e. the fixture's custody still holds another live lease at that moment. This is a genuine allocation-accounting boundary of the shared-bank work (E91), not a mere label problem, and it cannot be satisfied by loosening the assertion. No production code, assertion, budget or Native input was changed. | `checkpoint_retention_native173.cj:42-53`; `logical_json_payload.cj:152-164,196,247-254`; typing gate verified by probe at `detachReturned173` |

| E116 | Minimal host gate passes on the working source | The repository's own `scripts/minimal_host_gate.py` runs to completion and reports PASS with all four stages: `source_materialized_without_optional_business`, `actual_minimal_executable_built`, `actual_registry_and_sqlite_source_inventory_passed`, and `fixed_binary_offline_cli_blackbox_passed`. That materially covers the A2-04 "physically MCP-free Minimal dynamic install" and DoD-1 Minimal-completeness direction, though the independent MCP Client and Server components (A2-01/A2-02) are separate and remain open. This is a real product-artifact result for the Minimal profile, not a source scan. It does not by itself cover the Coding or Full distributions, the MCP dual-component flows, or the remaining `agent_product` boundaries, and those stay open. | `minimal-gate-run/summary.json`; `minimal-gate-run/blackbox.log`; `minimal-gate-run/build.log` |

| E118 | Exact per-iteration retention of the bank-pressure loop, attributed to the host handshake payload budgets | From the captured acquisition sequence, each loop iteration permanently adds exactly **33,950,072 bytes**, the same figure E74 recorded, made of one charge of 16,777,216 and one of 17,172,856 (= 16,777,216 + 395,640). The captured stacks record the acquisitions on `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget.acquire` reached through `LogicalJsonProcessBudget.acquire` → `NativePayloadProcessBudget.acquire` from `JavaScriptExtensionHost.readMessage` during `completePluginHostHandshake` (the per-host payload budget created at `agent_extension_runtime/src/javascript_host.cj:439` and acquired with `request.maxFrameBytes` at `:1475`), while the alternating cost deltas of 16,777,216 and 17,172,856 show one fresh 16 MiB floor plus one ~395,640 growth on an existing reservation per iteration. These records are *acquire-entered* observations rather than proof of final ownership, and `readMessage` closes its own charge in a `finally`, so the exact reservation that fails to close still needs a close-side instrument on the host-close path; what is certain is the net 33,950,072 per mount and that it is this payload-budget family rather than the Registry custody. Because `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget.acquire` charges `max(16777216, bytes * 8)`, a 262144-byte frame still pays the full 16 MiB floor, so two budgets per mount cost about 32 MiB plus their 395,640 of management. The bank holds only 134,217,728, so four mounts exhaust it and the fifth fails with `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted` at the checksum `charged=118627432` before the refusal — exactly the observed failure. `readMessage` does close its own charge in a `finally`, so the surviving charges are not those transient ones. A probe on the Registry custody rule shows that path is NOT the leak: `PluginRegistryMetadataCustody.finishLocked` observes `costs`/`payments` draining 12→0 and then `rootClosed=true` with both empty, so the custody allocation does release. The retained family is therefore the per-host `NativePayloadProcessBudget` created at `javascript_host.cj:439`, whose every `acquire` charges the shared bank a fresh 16 MiB floor (`logical_json_payload.cj:258-273` → `:41-51`) and whose reservations are held by the mount's retained raw aliases, consistent with the E79 finding that no safe retirement witness exists for those aliases. Distinguishing these two families is what makes the remaining fix tractable: the custody side is already correct. This identifies the concrete reservation family to bound; it is a multi-file lifetime change and no code, assertion, budget or Native input was changed here. | `bounded-source-fields-and-final-identity-audit.json` acquisitions 1-10; `javascript_host.cj:439,1475`; `logical_json_payload.cj:41-51,258-273` |

| E119 | Both MCP plugin components verified as independent packages on the working source | The two MCP components are separate cjpm packages with their own manifests, entry points and protocol harnesses: `plugins/mcp` (`mcp_client_plugin`, "Independent MCP client service using host-authorized byte channels") and `plugins/mcp_server` (`mcp_server_plugin`, "Independent powerless MCP server decoder for generic host stdio ingress"). The MCP client harness ran against the gate-built package `mcp-plugin-9n8shuqy` and passed **51/51** tests in 98.8 seconds, including the logical-payload controls (three-MiB input with a complete six-MiB result, forged artifact identity, overlap/offset/digest/cross-invocation rejection). The MCP server was rebuilt from its own source by `build-package.sh` (`cjpm build success`) into a fresh package and its harness then passed **20/20** actual process cases. Because each harness drives its own packaged executable over stdio and the gate builds both packages independently, this is real component-level evidence for the A2-01 and A2-02 directions. It does not by itself cover the host-side dual-component lifecycle, the original nine Product acceptance cases, discovery/schema/interaction/cancellation (A2-03) or the built MCP-free Minimal claim (A2-04), which remain open. | `plugins/mcp/check.py` run (51/51); `plugins/mcp_server/check.py` run (20/20); `mcpserver-build` |

| E120 | Decisive acquire/release trace: each mount leaks exactly one 16 MiB bank reservation | Instrumenting `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget.acquire` and `release` gives the exact sequence for the loop consumer. Every iteration performs two acquires - `bytes=4096` (the Registry metadata custody, cost 16,777,216) and `bytes=262144` (the handshake payload budget, cost 16,777,216) - but only **one** release of 16,777,216. The running `charged` therefore ends each iteration higher by exactly one floor: 16,777,216 then 33,508,864 then 50,286,880 then 67,011,136 then 83,788,352 then 100,512,608 then 117,289,824, and the next acquire (which would need 117,289,824 + 16,777,216 = 134,067,040 within a 134,217,728 ceiling) fails with `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted`. So the leak is one un-released 16 MiB floor per mount rather than thirty-three megabytes of distinct data, and the `count` ledger confirms at most five live reservations at any time. Because each mount creates a fresh `PluginRegistryMetadataCustody` whose probe showed it draining to zero and closing, the surviving reservation is on the handshake/payload-budget side, skipped when the mount carries retained raw aliases - the same no-safe-retirement-witness condition E79 established. This is the tightest available statement of the blocking item without changing code. | `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget` acquire/release trace (probe reverted); E91/E118 acquisition records |

| E121 | Root-cause identification: the host handshake payload charge is acquired and never released in this loop | Tagging the two candidate sites and the custody release path shows the pairing unambiguously. The Registry side is healthy: the trace contains `custody_release` for essentially every custody instance (ids 8,9,11,12,15,16,75,76,80,81,83,84,87,88,91,93,94,95,100,102,103,104) plus a `custody_init`, i.e. custody allocations are created and closed normally. The handshake side is where the pairing breaks: the trace contains one `hs_acquire maxFrame=262144` and **zero** `hs_release`, even though `readMessage` closes its charge in a `finally`. Note the counts do not yet pair up with E120's per-iteration 262144 acquires - E120 observed two 262144 acquires per iteration while this tagged run recorded a single handshake acquire overall - so more than one site acquires a 262144 payload budget and the tagged `readMessage` is only one of them. The `readMessage` charge itself is therefore not proven to be the leaked one; what is proven is that the 262144-entry payload-budget family acquires on several paths while at least one of its closes is skipped. Because the shared bank charges a full 16,777,216 floor per acquire regardless of the 262144 request, that single un-released charge is exactly the one-floor-per-iteration leak measured in E120, and it exhausts the 134,217,728 ceiling around the fifth mount. So the blocking item is narrow and precise: the handshake/host-side payload reservation opened during `completePluginHostHandshake` is not returned when the mount ends with retained raw aliases, matching the E79 no-safe-retirement-witness condition. No code, assertion, budget or Native input was changed; probes were reverted byte-exactly. | tagged `custody_init`/`custody_release` and `hs_acquire`/`hs_release` trace (probes reverted); E118/E120 |

| E124 | Two compile defects in the PR-added `agent_core_contract` support test, fixed | Running the named `vnext_contract_gate` stage failed before any behaviour ran: `build failed to compile package agent_core_contract` with four errors. Two were `undeclared identifier 'executionUsageValue'` at `support_tests/agent_core_contract/src/context_cut_recovery.cj:241` (twice) plus a dependent lambda-annotation and arity error, because `executionUsageValue` is package-private in `agent_domain/src/v3_control.cj:545`; the file already imports `agent_domain.*`, and the domain publishes the equivalent `public func contextBoundaryUsageValue(value: ExecutionUsage): AgentValue` at `agent_domain/src/context_boundary.cj:77`, so the test simply named the wrong function. The fourth error was `missing argument for parameter list` at `durable_trace_contract.cj:723`, where `resumeContextBoundary` is called with one argument although its signature is `(request, sink)` (`agent_core/src/core.cj:339`) and every other caller passes an event sink. Both were corrected minimally - the three call sites now use the public `contextBoundaryUsageValue`, and the call passes `{ _ => () }` as its sink - with no assertion value, input or expectation changed. After the fix the package compiles (`cjpm build success`) and the contract runs its real behavioural checks. | `vnext_contract_gate` output before/after; `agent_domain/src/v3_control.cj:545`; `context_boundary.cj:77`; `core.cj:339` |

| E125 | Behavioural failure behind the contract compile fixes: the safe-cut executor panics on its retained context | With `agent_core_contract` compiling, its own `coreCatalogSafeCutContracts` still fails: `IllegalStateException: contract failed: the already-requested first tool keeps its original executor despite concurrent directory publication` (`catalog_safe_cut.cj:126`). Instrumentation narrows it precisely - the Core run returns an error whose code is `tool.executor_panicked` ("tool executor escaped its Result boundary"), with `first.contexts.size == 0` and only one model request recorded, so the failure happens on the first tool dispatch rather than after a successful cut. A further probe captures the exact panic: the executor is entered and `context.retainInvocationContext159()` throws `escaping Context has no prepaid DATA owner`, i.e. the `case None` branch of `retainInvocationContext159Action159` where a context has neither an `ownedToolSnapshot159` nor an `ownedInvocationData180` to derive a retained child from (`tool_runtime/src/context.cj:609-645`). A further probe confirms the context reports `hasOwnedToolSetSnapshot159=false` and `hasOwnedInvocationData180=false` at that point. Production never retains in that state: `hostRetainLiveInvocationContext159` returns None up front when neither owner is present (`agent_host_runtime/src/host_live_invocation_context159.cj:80-81`), and the managed-channel helpers retain only `if (owned)` (`host_managed_channel_effect_owner.cj:11`, `host_managed_channel_owned_session.cj:16`). The reason this fixture has no owner is that its run resolves to the legacy lane: `coreModelLane` uses `catalog.sharedToolSetSnapshot159(...)` and only falls back to `coreLegacyModelLane159`, which constructs a `ModelLane` from a plain `ToolSetSnapshot` and therefore leaves `ownedToolSet159` unset (`agent_core/src/core.cj:9717-9741,9742-9776`; `model_lane.cj:339`), i.e. this catalog has no Registry-owned data. So the contract forces an unguarded retention on a legacy lane that production code would have declined, and the retention path correctly refuses. The remaining decision is what the intended contract actually is: either the Core legacy lane must be able to supply an owned tool-set snapshot - today the only implementation of `ToolCatalogSnapshotPayment159` is the Host class `HostRegistryCatalogIdentity159` (`agent_host_runtime/src/registry_metadata_copy.cj:513`), so a plain `ToolCatalog()` such as this fixture's can never be registry-backed and `coreModelLane` will always take the legacy branch - or the supported way to observe a frozen executor after a safe cut is to capture the snapshot value rather than retain the context, as other frames in this contract do with `budget.snapshot()`/`pool.snapshot()`. Both readings change product or contract semantics, so neither was applied mechanically. That makes the next question specific: whether the contract's `CutContextTool` is supposed to receive such an owner from the Core dispatch path (in which case the Core dispatch is missing the owner), or whether the contract should not call `retainInvocationContext159()` on every dispatch. Resolving it needs a decision on the intended contract rather than a mechanical edit, so no production or test change was made. This contract file was added by this PR's own commit `158f11ee`, so it is a real outstanding behavioural boundary of the migration rather than a stale fixture. Probes were reverted byte-exactly and no production code, assertion, budget or Native input was changed. | `vnext_contract_gate` run output; `tool.executor_panicked` capture; `catalog_safe_cut.cj:106-129`; `context.cj:604-660,988-991` |

Addressing it that way then returns **`tool.preparation_not_executed`**, which is defined as a deliberate Direct-mode rejection: `rejectedDirectPreparationOutput()` in `agent_runtime/src/direct_preparation_rejection.cj` returns exactly `tool.preparation_not_executed` with the message that the Direct tool did not execute because preparation did not complete. Direct invocation is therefore the wrong entry point for a prepare-gated plugin tool; passing tests drive such tools through `ApplicationSession.invokeJavaScriptPluginCommand` with the `plugin.<namespace>.<name>` route (`agent_product/src/plugin_command_control_test.cj:46-47`) or through an agent run, and the archived-bytes assertion in `plugin_continuation_payload_test.cj:15-26` shows the same tools declaring `preparation_provider: prepare_document`. All diagnostic edits were reverted: the helper, the namespaced name and the extra install binding are gone (`grep` for `promptRequireTool`/`memoryNamespace` is empty), the package still builds, and the committed `prepare_document` fixture change is intact. The remaining work on this contract is therefore to invoke the Memory tool through the prepared path rather than Direct, not to hunt an undefined value. | run logs `d2run.log` (`tool.not_found`), `d4run.log` (`tool.preparation_not_executed`); `agent_runtime/src/direct_preparation_rejection.cj`; `agent_product/src/plugin_command_control_test.cj:46-47` |

| E140 | The 16 MiB per-iteration retention now has a named owner, and it is a real missing-release path | E120/E121 could show the charge but not who held it. Following the canonical projection custody to its payer names the holder. `agent_host_runtime/src/paid_value_projection151.cj:214 publicationLease151()` mints the `ToolResultPayloadLease` that pays the projection, with `retirePublished151()` (`:225`) as its release. In the owned canonical readback path `agent_core/src/core.cj:4375` captures exactly that lease, and the commit path hands the SAME lease instance to the runtime at `:4387` via `control.runtime.takeOwnedCanonicalRetirement(capture.lease.getOrThrow())`. The holder is `OwnedCanonicalRetirementIndex` (`agent_runtime/src/owned_canonical_result_retirement.cj:26-40`): its `records` list retains every `OwnedCanonicalResultRetirement` (`:9-19`), each of which keeps the paying `ToolResultPayloadLease` in a private field (`:13`), while `prepay` charges the same bank for the metadata. A record leaves that list only when a later call matches the lease by reference identity (`matches(actual)` `:19`; `takeOwnedCanonicalRetirement` `thread_coordinator.cj:605-614`). So a canonical result that commits but whose lease instance is never passed back - or is passed back as a different instance - keeps its roughly 16 MiB charge for the life of the thread; `OwnedCanonicalResultRetirement` has no close/release method at all, so no other route can retire it. The fix therefore belongs in the commit/consume path (`commitOwnedParentOutcome`/`consumeRetainedOwnedParentOutcome` at `core.cj:4300-4345` and the readback path at `4380-4395`), not in `libs/logical_json_payload`: every committed canonical result's exact lease must reach `takeOwnedCanonicalRetirement`, and records whose lease was retired by another route need a bounded release. This is the counted private-claim ownership design the blocking item calls for, now anchored to specific lines rather than to the bank in general. | `agent_host_runtime/src/paid_value_projection151.cj:214,225`; `agent_core/src/core.cj:4375,4387`; `agent_runtime/src/owned_canonical_result_retirement.cj:9-40`; `agent_runtime/src/thread_coordinator.cj:605-614` |

| E141 | Confirmed product regression: the platform request path no longer injects any active goal, and this one is precisely attributable | `agent_product/src/plugin_workflow_document_test.cj` line 91 asserts `request.system.contains("Finish autonomously")`, so an active goal is required to reach the request system prompt; `goal_contract` asserts the same at its lines 90-97. At the base commit the wiring existed: `git show 4d88fa60:agent_product/src/product.cj` lines 543-546 computed `let activeGoal = match (goalRuntime.activePrompt(sessionId)) { ... }` and passed that non-empty string as `buildProductPrompt`'s second argument. In the current tree `requestPromptBuildResult` (`product.cj:565-582`) passes a literal `""` for that same parameter, with a comment stating that request-time business context now arrives through the configured Native ContextProvider and canonical InitialContextPreparation. That replacement path does exist and is the intended design: a version-5 `plugins/context.json` selection names a provider whose contribution lands in the request's context snapshot (`plugin_initial_context.cj:45-56,137-142`, `plugin_context_provider.cj:120-207`), and the workflow package ships that provider - `plugins/workflow/src/goal_context.cj` validates the `_axyndra_context_provider` identity and returns `axyndra.context-result/v1` contributions (`:12-19,60`). The goal surface also exposes exact equivalents of the two helpers this replaced: the goal tool accepts a `method` field (`goal_document.cj:32-33`) dispatched in `plugins/workflow/src/policy.cj:121-159`, giving `method=prompt` for the old `activePrompt` and `method=restore` for `pauseRestoredActive`. What is therefore NOT established is whether the product actually configures and exercises that provider for the ordinary request path, because nothing in this tree was found wiring a `goal_context` selection by default and the prompt section itself is still rendered from the `activeGoal` parameter (`prompt_runtime.cj:16-22,76-88`). So the correct statement is a verification gap on a real mechanism, not a missing mechanism: the contract must prove the goal text reaches the model's system input through whichever path is canonical, rather than assume the old parameter still carries it. | `agent_product/src/product.cj:565-582` (current `""`); `git show 4d88fa60:agent_product/src/product.cj` (lines 543-546, 562-564); `agent_product/src/prompt_runtime.cj:16-22,76-88`; `agent_product/src/plugin_workflow_document_test.cj:91`; `plugins/workflow/src/goal_context.cj:60` |

| E142 | The retained-canonical path has no retirement take-site; whether that is a leak or the designed payload cache needs the design owner | `OwnedCanonicalRetirementIndex.prepay` runs on EVERY owned parent commit (`agent_runtime/src/thread_coordinator.cj:1508-1512`, guarded only by `ownedCanonicalParent.isSome() && ownedCanonicalHistory.isSome()`), adding one `OwnedCanonicalResultRetirement` to `records` and charging the same bank for its metadata. But in the whole production tree there is EXACTLY ONE `takeOwnedCanonicalRetirement` call site: `agent_core/src/core.cj:4387`, inside `consumeOwnedCanonicalReadbackOutcome`. The sibling `consumeRetainedOwnedParentOutcome` (`core.cj:4300-4345`) prepares its backing, commits the canonical attempt, closes after consumption and releases its ack debt, but never takes a retirement record - so every commit delivered through that path leaves a record holding its paying `ToolResultPayloadLease` for the life of the thread. Two readings remain, and they are not distinguishable from the code alone: either this is a missing release on the retained path (the record should be retired once its consumer is done, as the readback path does), or it is deliberate - the class doc calls itself a 'resource retirement' ledger that exists so the payload can still be read back after the coordinator removed the original result DOM, which would make permanent retention intentional and shift the question to whether the 8-owner aggregate ceiling (8 x 16 MiB against the 128 MiB bank) is the right bound. I did not change either side: the new real-ish fix belongs to whoever owns that design intent, and a wrong patch here would either leak the payload or break canonical readback. The verifiable fact recorded is the asymmetry: prepay on every owned commit, take on exactly one of the two consume paths. | `agent_runtime/src/thread_coordinator.cj:1508-1512`; `agent_core/src/core.cj:4387` (sole take) vs `:4300-4345` (no take); `agent_runtime/src/owned_canonical_result_retirement.cj:26-40` |

| E145 | Fixed a real production defect: the workflow goal context provider rejected every real installed package identity | The Host supplies the document context provider's identity from the frozen policy binding, whose digest is the real `PluginPackageDescriptor` content identity. `plugins/workflow/src/goal_context.cj` validated that field with `httpIntentDigest`, which accepts only the raw 64-hex form (`agent_domain/src/http_service_intent.cj:322`), while the Host supplies the tagged `sha256:<64hex>` form that `httpServicePackageDigest` accepts (`:123-125`). The fix accepts either form with the same note that this is a descriptive input check only and that actual ownership is verified by Host/Store from the frozen provider and the canonical fact. This is production code, not a contract fixture: it is the first real product bug found by the contract-migration work rather than by the bank investigation. | `plugins/workflow/src/goal_context.cj:18-25` (fix, commit 3220506a); `plugins/memory/src/native_context_provider.cj:18-20`; `agent_domain/src/http_service_intent.cj:123-125,322`; `plugins/workflow/cjpm build success` |

| E146 | The bank exhaustion has a precise mechanism: one process-wide budget serves BOTH the unreleased canonical leases AND every observed context query, each with a 16 MiB floor | `agent_extension_runtime/src/native_payload.cj:18` declares a single process-wide `let nativePayloadOwnerBudget = LogicalJsonOwnerBudget()`, and every reservation takes from it through the same `acquire`, whose cost is `max(16777216, bytes * 8)` (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:42`) against a 128 MiB aggregate (`:28`). Two different consumers both reserve from it: (1) canonical result payloads, via the projection lease holder described in E140/E142/E143; and (2) observed context queries - `HostFrozenPluginContentQuery(query, selectedIds, observed: true)` calls `reserveNativeContentJson(query, CONTENT_PAYLOAD_INPUT_BYTES)` (`agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_owner_helpers.cj:20-22`), which is `nativePayloadOwnerBudget.acquire(logicalJsonWriteReservationBytes(...))` (`native_payload.cj:180-182`), with `CONTENT_PAYLOAD_INPUT_BYTES = CONTENT_PAYLOAD_CONTEXT_SNAPSHOT_BYTES + 262144 = 4194304 + 262144 = 4456448` (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/content_payload_limits.cj:11-13`). Because of the 16 MiB floor, EACH observed context query therefore consumes at least 16 MiB of the same 128 MiB, so at most eight such holders can coexist; canonical result leases that are never retired (E142) occupy the same slots. That is why the blocking item presents as `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted` on the loop representative and as `context.query_invalid` on the request path: same bank, same floor, two different wrappers. | `agent_extension_runtime/src/native_payload.cj:18,30-32,180-182`; `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:28,42`; `libs/logical_json_payload/src/content_payload_limits.cj:11-13`; `agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_owner_helpers.cj:20-22`; `agent_product/src/plugin_context_provider.cj:89-90` |

| E152 | Instrumented the shared bank and identified the dominant consumer: ONE reservation that grows by shared-custody calls and is never closed | Temporary probes on `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget.acquire/release/acquireFixedBuffer/grow` plus a per-reservation identity printed on construction, on every `ensureRetainedPeak` growth and on close, run against `goal_contract` (all probes reverted; `libs/logical_json_payload` is byte-identical to its committed state). The reservation identity probes show that same reservation (the first one created) is the one that grew, and it never prints a close. Because `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget.grow` is called from `LogicalJsonOwnerReservation.ensureRetainedPeak` (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:190-192`), which is the method the shared-custody `ensure` path drives, this is the derived-child growth pattern from E115: children of a shared custody raise the parent owner's charge without a matching release, so the bank's `charged` climbs to 85,153,376 bytes of growth while `count` stays at 1. The aggregate therefore fills from a single owner's growth, not from many holders, which is why the per-owner arithmetic in E120/E121 did not add up and why the seat is exhausted before the sixth context read. The consequence for the repair is precise: releasing already-closed reservations is NOT what is missing (65 of 66 release) - what is missing is a ceiling or a release for the growth that shared-custody children add to their parent. | probe logs `gc-probe.log`, `gc-probe2.log`; reverted file verified byte-identical to `HEAD`; `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:190-192`; `agent_runtime/src/owned_canonical_result_retirement.cj` (E151) |

| E153 | The bank's real defect is that ONE owner can ratchet to nearly the whole aggregate: growth is unbounded per owner | E152 measured 8,797 `grow` calls and one surviving reservation; the same probe data identifies its origin - the first bank acquire in the run is `bytes=4096` (`PROBEBANK ACQ cost=16777216 bytes=4096`), which is `reserveNativeControlMetadata(4096)`, i.e. canonical projection custody (`agent_product/src/canonical_projection_route173.cj:131`, `owned_canonical_projection186.cj:121`), and the survivor is that same first reservation. Reading the accounting explains why one owner alone can fill an 128 MiB pool: `LogicalJsonOwnerReservation.ensureRetainedPeak` raises `cost` monotonically with NO ceiling (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:190-192`, `if (minimum > cost) { owner.grow(minimum - cost); cost = minimum }`), and both shared-custody entry points do the same to their parent - `derive` grows the parent by each new child's slot (`:150`) and `ensure` grows it by every child's retained peak increase (`:158-160`). Nothing bounds the SUM a single owner may accumulate, and nothing returns it until the LAST lease closes (`close(id)` only calls `owner.release(cost)` when `leases.isEmpty()`, `:170`). So an owner that lives long and mints thousands of children - a projection/conversion custody, a configuration cache, or the retirement index - ratchets the shared bank to its aggregate limit by itself; that is why the earlier per-owner arithmetic (E120/E121, max eight owners) did not predict the failure and why the pool is exhausted before the sixth context read. The repair is therefore a per-owner (or per-custody-sum) growth ceiling and/or releasing child slots with their real users, NOT lowering the 16 MiB floor or raising the 128 MiB cap; both of those remain protected. | probe logs `gc-probe.log`, `gc-probe2.log` (probes reverted; both `libs/logical_json_payload` and the retirement file verified against their intended states, and the inherited uncommitted work in `logical_json_payload.cj` was restored after an accidental `git checkout`); `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:150,158-160,170,190-192`; `agent_product/src/canonical_projection_route173.cj:131` |

This is the strongest single piece of evidence for the blocking item: the unbounded per-owner bank growth of E153 does not merely stop three contract packages, it takes out roughly three quarters of the product package's own tests, so fixing it is the highest-leverage change available and nothing else in the remaining work can be honestly declared verified while it stands. | `agent_product` test run under the gate env: `TOTAL 1038, PASSED 200, ERROR 827, FAILED 11`; 789 x `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted` |

| E155 | Probe hygiene, including one self-inflicted error that was detected and repaired | The E152/E153 bank probes were removed before recording, and the affected file was verified: `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj` now differs from `HEAD` exactly as it did before this session's probes (43 insertions / 25 deletions of inherited owner-first work), no `PROBE`/`PRIVATE_DIAG` string remains anywhere in the `.cj` tree, and `agent_product` and `agent_core` both build again. Restoring from `HEAD` therefore destroyed that work and broke the build with four errors in `logical_json_payload.cj`. It was recovered from the pre-probe backup taken at the start of the instrumentation, which contained the complete inherited state and no probes. The lesson is recorded because it is a real hazard of this tree: with hundreds of inherited uncommitted files, `git checkout`/`git restore` on a file is destructive, and the safe revert is a pre-instrumentation copy, not the committed version. | pre/post diff of `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj` (43/25, unchanged content); `grep -rl "PROBE\|PRIVATE_DIAG" --include=*.cj .` empty; builds `ap7.log` (`cjpm build success`) and `core1.log` |

| E156 | The ratchet's exact site and the exact fix point, including why the fix is a design decision rather than a local edit | The ~8,000 grows of roughly 1,500-3,000 bytes per run come from the subscription dispatch path: `ApplicationOwnedSubscription113.deriveDispatchPayment113` (`agent_product/src/application_owned_subscription113.cj:78-93`) calls `fundChildRegistrationLocked113`, which grows the owner whenever the registration peak rises (`:73-75`), and then `paid.deriveRetainedLease()` + `child.ensureRetainedPeak(...)`, each of which also grows the same owner. It is driven once per dispatch frame by `application.cj:2032-2038` with a peak computed from the subscriber count (`8192 + count*512`). The children DO retire - `endRetirableDispatch113` pairs every frame with `retireAfterHelper113` -> `subscriptionPaymentCloseHelper113` -> `child.close()` (`:2050-2063`, `:228-238`), and `collectAcknowledgedLocked113` prunes the lists. A subscription owner that lives for the whole session therefore ratchets its `cost` to the peak concurrency and holds it against the 128 MiB aggregate for the session's life - which is exactly what the probe measured (one owner, 85 MiB of growth, `count=1`). The targeted fix is to return the owner's excess above its live `sum` when a child closes while keeping the 16 MiB floor (i.e. a release inside `LogicalJsonSharedCustody.close`), or to bound per-owner growth. Both are edits to protected accounting inside `libs/logical_json_payload`, which other invariants read directly - the maintenance-failure test asserts `nativeControlMetadataUsage113()[1] == 134217728` exactly (`agent_product/src/checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:74-76,106`) - so changing it silently could break those assertions. It is recorded here with this precision rather than patched unilaterally, because the 16 MiB floor and the 128 MiB aggregate are protected values for this task and their accounting is the design owner's call. | `agent_product/src/application_owned_subscription113.cj:73-93,228-238`; `agent_product/src/application.cj:2032-2063,5016`; `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:150,158-160,170,190-192`; `agent_product/src/checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:74-76,106`; probe logs `gc-probe.log`, `gc-probe2.log` |

| E157 | Implemented the bounded excess-release for shared-custody owners, and it breaks the bank blocker without touching either protected value | E156 named the site; this is the bounded repair. | E159 | After the shared-custody fix, `product_prompt_contract` no longer fails on the bank in its own setup; the remaining failure is package import, and the bank head-room in that run was only 16.8 MB | Re-running the contract against the rebuilt product with the E157 fix, its failure point MOVED: it no longer reports `thread.durable_commit_failed: logical payload aggregate decoded capacity is exhausted` on the third ContextRead, it now stops earlier in `promptCreateGoal` with `workflow goal component import failed` (`installed_context.cj:115-118`), i.e. before any request is made. Because these runs cannot be treated as independent evidence while another process shares that package, the honest conclusion is that the fix's direct effect is established only for `goal_contract` (E157), which reached and passed its registered-schema stage, and that `product_prompt_contract` must be re-measured serially before any conclusion about it. | run log `ppc-probe2.log` (`workflow goal component import failed` at `promptCreateGoal`); bank instrumented run in the same log (`charged=16867328 count=1`, 106 events); probes reverted (`diff` vs `cp` backup clean, `grep` for probes empty) |

| E160 | Recovered the masked cause of `tool.preparation_not_executed`: it is the shared bank again, and this is the THIRD site of the same error-masking pattern | The E157-fixed product moved `goal_contract` past its schema stage into `verifyDurableLifecycle`, where it failed with `tool.preparation_not_executed`. A GDB breakpoint on the conversion site in the actual contract process recovered the true inner error: `extension.protocol_failed` / `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted`, raised in `start()` before any ContextRead admission (the SQLite state shows only a rejected parent). So the failure was never a missing preparation or a Direct-vs-prepared routing mistake - that premise was wrong, and my earlier guidance to the agent was corrected in-flight. The masking happens at `agent_host_runtime/src/direct_preparation_flow.cj:253 rejectParent(cause)`, which builds the generic `rejectedDirectPreparationOutput()` and only propagates the cause when `cause.kind == AgentErrorKind.Cancelled` (`:260`); every other cause - including a bank exhaustion - is replaced by the opaque deterministic rejection. This is now the third instance of the same anti-pattern in this migration, alongside `plugin_context_provider.cj:89-90` (four causes collapsed into `context.query_invalid`, E146) and `application.cj:2450-2452` (`application.owned_failure_pending` discarding the real exception, E104). Named as a pattern rather than three unrelated defects because each one turned a resource or accounting failure into an innocent-looking validation result, and each cost real diagnosis time. The contract-side cause is separately identified: the contract retains its charged canonical ToolResults across the product close/reopen, so its own held leases exhaust the bank it shares. | GDB-recovered cause in the contract process (`extension.protocol_failed` / `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted`); `agent_host_runtime/src/direct_preparation_flow.cj:253,260`; `agent_product/src/plugin_context_provider.cj:89-90`; `agent_product/src/application.cj:2450-2452` |

| E161 | The retained-result family is a designed trade, not a stray leak; releasing it costs losing the in-memory result DOM | Two measurements falsified the natural fixes. First, the agent's discriminator: the contract's own 5-8 live `ToolResult`s were all released before close, and the public bank reading stayed EXACTLY `(4, 86180928)` -> `(4, 86180928)`, with the failure unchanged - because `ToolResultPayloadLease.close()` (`agent_domain/src/tool_result_payload.cj:13-17`) is NOT the canonical result's release. Second, reading the ownership shows why: the record's lease comes from the committed parent itself (`thread_coordinator.cj:1563 let lease = ownedCanonicalParent.getOrThrow().actualPayloadLease()`, `owned_canonical_parent_commit.cj:27` = `output.payloadLease`), so the charge is held by `OwnedCanonicalRetirementIndex.records` from commit time, and the record must stay in `records` precisely so `CoreOwnedCanonicalEventDelivery`/`CoreOwnedCanonicalObserverDelivery` can `visitCommittedItem` and re-read the canonical item later (`core.cj:4406-4416`). That is the designed reason the comment at `thread_coordinator.cj:1559-1562` gives for removing the coordinator's big alias and moving the result DOM here. My E151 `record.close()` therefore closes the wrong thing: it retires the lease AFTER the deliveries, i.e. after the record was already taken and is already being released by the consume route, which is why it changes no observable reading (the agent measured no difference from it either) and why it only risks breaking a later readback. The honest conclusion is that this family cannot be released without either keeping every committed result in memory for the thread's life or giving up readback-driven delivery; both are design decisions that belong to the migration's owner, exactly as the zero-REASON disciplines recorded elsewhere. What remains actionable and non-designed is only the per-owner growth ceiling of E153, which the E157 `shrink` already bounds. | agent discriminator: usage `(4,86180928)` -> `(4,86180928)` unchanged, restored call still failing, before-restored `(3,106016056)`; `agent_runtime/src/thread_coordinator.cj:1559-1564`; `agent_runtime/src/owned_canonical_parent_commit.cj:27`; `agent_core/src/core.cj:4406-4416` |

| E162 | The E157 `shrink` violated a tested invariant and was reverted; the invariant is now documented at the code | E157's bounded excess-release was wrong, and `agent_domain` caught it: `DerivedDataPhasesSumEveryActualPeakInTheOriginalSingleBank` (`agent_domain/src/logical_json_paid_phase_328_test.cj:8-22`) asserts that after two derived phases grow to 64 MiB and 32 MiB and are then CLOSED, the bank still reports `usage()[1] == 100663296 + 524288 + 3072` with `usage()[0] == 1`, and its own comment states `Shared high water remains paid until actual original closes`. Releasing the excess on child close therefore under-counts the aggregate the single-bank contract guarantees. Reverting restored that package exactly: `agent_domain` went from `PASSED 173 / FAILED 1` back to **`PASSED 174 / FAILED 0`**. Rather than leave the behaviour undocumented, the invariant is now stated in the code where the next reader will meet it (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj`, above the `leases.isEmpty()` release), so the same wrong fix is not attempted a third time. Consequence for the blocking item: the per-owner growth that E152/E153 measured is NOT reclaimable without weakening the single-bank peak contract, so the shared-bank item is bounded by design on both sides - E161 showed the retained-result family is a designed trade, and this shows the derived-child sum is a tested invariant. | `agent_domain/src/logical_json_paid_phase_328_test.cj:8-22`; `agent_domain` test runs 173/1 then 174/0 (`domain-test.log`, `domain-test2.log`); `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj` (documented invariant, commit ecd77058) |

| E164 | Serial re-measure: `product_prompt_contract` is still blocked by the bank, and the failure is reproducible | E159 left open whether that package's `workflow goal component import failed` was a package-path collision from concurrent runs. Re-run with nothing else executing, it does not reproduce that way: the contract gets past `promptCreateGoal` and fails at its first fixture request with `thread.durable_commit_failed: logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted` (`main.cj:180`), i.e. the same bank exhaustion as before the E151 change, so the E151 retirement release did not help this path either. Two bank releases are now disproved by measurement or by test, and one (E151) proven ineffective here, so this contract cannot complete while the shared pool holds the committed-result and derived-peak charges that E161/E162 establish as designed. | serial run log `ppc-serial.log` (`thread.durable_commit_failed: logical payload aggregate decoded capacity is exhausted` at `main.cj:180`); contrast `ppc-probe2.log` (E159 import failure while another run shared the package directory) |

| E165 | The single-bank high-water sum is a design invariant of the draft, not an incidental test, and the capacity arithmetic follows from it | Every candidate release of the shared-bank growth was evaluated and each is refused by design. E161 proves the committed-result leases are held on purpose so the canonical item can be re-read for event/observer delivery; E162 proves the derived-child high-water sum is asserted by the draft's own invariant (`agent_domain/src/logical_json_paid_phase_328_test.cj:8-22`, committed in `158f11ee`: two derived phases that grow the bank and then close leave `usage()[1] == 100663296 + 524288 + 3072` and `usage()[0] == 1`, with the comment that shared high water stays paid until the actual original closes). With that rule held, the arithmetic is forced: each reservation costs `max(16777216, bytes*8)` (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:42`), so A concurrent live reservations cost A x 16 MiB, and observed maxima of 4 concurrent reservations plus one ratcheting owner reach 118,740,576 (`gc-probe.log`); the six context reads plus the projection and retirement owners that a single request needs therefore require about 112-128 MiB against the fixed 128 MiB aggregate (`:28`). That is why the same message appears in three independent consumers and why no release can be added without breaking either E161's readback or E162's assertion. The honest conclusion for these two contracts is that the shared-bank ceiling on the current design leaves them no room inside the protected 16 MiB floor and 128 MiB aggregate, and that widening either is a product decision outside this task's authorization. This is recorded as the terminal analysis of the item, not as a fix. | `agent_domain/src/logical_json_paid_phase_328_test.cj:8-22` (in `158f11ee`); `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:28,42`; capacity arithmetic from `gc-probe.log` (`count=4`, `charged=118740576`); E151/E157/E161/E162 |

| E166 | AUTHORIZED capacity fix: the aggregate is now sized to the pool's own concurrency bound, and `goal_contract` passes end to end | With the user's explicit authorization to raise the 16 MiB floor or the 128 MiB aggregate, the aggregate was raised and the floor left alone. The chosen value is principled rather than arbitrary: `maximumTransfers` is 32 and each reservation costs at least the 16 MiB floor (`libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:42`), so the pool's own declared concurrency needs 32 x 16 MiB = **512 MiB**; a 128 MiB aggregate could not admit even a quarter of the transfers it advertises, which is exactly why ordinary multi-owner requests exhausted it. `maximumCharged` default is now `536870912` with the floor and transfer bound unchanged. Measured effect: **`goal_contract` now prints `goal contract passed` and exits 0** (previously unreachable), and `product_prompt_contract`'s bank error is gone - its first fixture request now COMPLETES and it fails later on `context.read_failed`. A `capacity()` numeric reader was added to the bank plus thin product/extension readers, and the three tests that had hardcoded the old aggregate now either derive from that reader (`checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj`) or pin the aggregate they were written against via `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget(maximumCharged: 134217728)`, so each invariant is now about the CONFIGURED capacity instead of an accidental default. `agent_domain` is back to **174 passed / 0 failed** and `product_prompt_contract`'s pool-pinning compiles. | `libs/logical_json_payload/src/logical_json_payload.cj:32` (512 MiB default) and `capacity()`; `agent_extension_runtime/src/native_payload.cj`; `agent_product/src/control_metadata113.cj`; `agent_product/src/checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj`; `agent_domain/src/logical_json_shared_custody_test.cj`, `agent_domain/src/logical_json_copied_display_test.cj`, `agent_domain/src/logical_json_copied_display_growth_test.cj`; run logs `cap-goal_contract.log` (`goal contract passed`), `cap-ppc3.log`, `domain-cap3.log` (174/0) |

| E167 | Measured: quadrupling the aggregate removes the contract blockers but barely moves the package suite, which pins the suite's real cause | With the 512 MiB aggregate the `agent_product` suite moved from `PASSED 200 / ERROR 827 / FAILED 11` to `PASSED 206 / ERROR 820 / FAILED 12`, and the `logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted` count went 789 -> 780. So a 4x capacity increase bought only about nine bank failures, which is the decisive evidence for E158's contamination model: the remaining bank failures are not a fixed shortfall that a larger ceiling can cover, they are unbounded accumulation across 1038 cases in ONE process against a process-wide singleton bank (`agent_extension_runtime/src/native_payload.cj:18`). Any capacity would eventually be exhausted by enough cases, so the honest instrument for that family is per-class or per-case isolation (already demonstrated: `MountedExecutionFrameRetirement173bTests` runs in isolation and fails on real assertions, not on the bank), not a larger number. The capacity raise is therefore justified by what it actually fixed - the two contract packages, one of which now passes end to end - and not by the suite count. | suite logs `ap-tests.log` (200/827/11, 789 bank) vs `ap-tests-cap.log` (206/820/12, 780 bank); `agent_extension_runtime/src/native_payload.cj:18` |

The remaining work on this contract is therefore plugin native-host launch/timing on a second session, which is separate from both the goal migration and the shared bank. | probe run `ppc-deny2.log` (`PROBE_DENY update: extension.handshake_timeout`); `agent_extension_runtime/src/javascript_host.cj:315,1895`; `agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_context_read.cj:360-361`; masking sites as listed |

| E182 | The `hub_process_read wait` DOES honour its timeout, so E179's 120-second hang is upstream of the wait loop - a narrower boundary than recorded | Tracing the wait to its real body refutes the natural reading of E179: `nativeProcessWait180` -> `nativeProcessWaitBody180` (`agent_product/src/process_supervisor.cj:1040-1068`) reads `timeout_ms`, records the start, and its polling loop breaks on `done` OR on `nowMillis()-started>=timeout` with `timedOut=true` (`:1063`), sleeping 10 ms per iteration. Combined with E182 (the wait loop IS bounded), the only remaining shape that explains 120 seconds of silence is a reservation primary that cannot be granted - i.e. the derived-process projection meter contending for the same shared bank the capacity fix already raised. This is stated as the leading hypothesis with its evidence, not as a conclusion: confirming it needs an instrumented run that prints the meter's `ensureRetainedPeak`/`fundAdditional151` values and the bank's `charged` at that moment, which is the next trace to run.
