So the honest position is: the App's wait implementation is exercised-and-correct in at least one real run, the failure needs the harness's particular concurrent stimulus (two owner labels contending for one broker socket with back-to-back send/wait), and reproducing that window deliberately is the next concrete step - not more standalone runs, which provably take the fast path. | `/tmp/wait-gdb.txt` (success with `timedOut: false`); five standalone runs all returning `state: recoveryRequired` short-circuits; `agent_product/src/process_supervisor.cj` `nativeProcessWaitBody180` recovery branch |

| E197 | The stall now has a DETERMINISTIC, minimal, out-of-harness reproducer, and the trigger is isolated to the `ps` op | Built the harness's exact sequence as a standalone script (import + primitives + enable + `hub_process start` with the harness's own bash script + then the read-family calls), each step a separate App invocation. Measurements, all with a fresh root and a worker sweep: **WITH `ps` the wait hangs (2/2)**; **WITHOUT `ps` the wait returns correctly (2/2)**; and **with `logs` as the first read call instead of `ps` the wait returns** - so the trigger is the `ps` op specifically, not "any earlier read", and not the two-owner labels the harness uses (my script is single-owner). The natural explanation from the code is that `ps` is the only observation that enumerates ALL daemons: `observeNativeProcess180`'s `op=="ps"` branch calls `fundNativeStateSnapshotLocked180` once per daemon plus `lease.retainData180(processInvoice180(daemons.size, 4096, 4096), daemons.size*4+8)` (`agent_product/src/process_supervisor.cj:812-820`), whereas `logs`/`describe`/`wait` touch only their named daemon's state - i.e. `ps` is the heaviest funded observation and the only one whose per-daemon funding is multiplied by the daemon count. This is where the shared-bank family (E146-E157) re-enters, but now with a reproducible handle and a single-line trigger instead of a vague contention claim. A full 9-call matrix (each read op as the first call, with and without `ps`) and a bank probe on the `ps` lease are the immediate next steps, and the script is retained at `the out-of-harness repro script` (+ `-nops`/`-logsfirst` variants) so they are cheap. Every claim here is an A/B with 2 repetitions per arm, per E190's discipline. | `the out-of-harness repro script` (with ps): 2/2 wait hang; `the repro script without `ps`` (no ps): 2/2 wait OK; `the repro script with `logs` first`: wait OK; `agent_product/src/process_supervisor.cj:812-820` (`ps` funds all daemons) vs `:826-822` (named-daemon ops) |

| E198 | Root cause identified as a concrete, provable defect: the process-observation lease is retained in a list that is NEVER drained | Following E197's deterministic `ps` trigger to the lease's lifecycle gives a defect rather than a vague pressure claim. In `agent_host_runtime/src/javascript_plugin_host.cj`, `registerActualProcessLease180` (`:82-89`) completes every process observation's payment handshake by doing `retainedProcessServices180.add(value)` and then CLEARING `pendingProcessPayment180`, i.e. the host deliberately keeps the actual lease (and therefore the paid projection behind it) after the invocation returns. `retainedProcessServices180` is declared at `:74` and appears in the ENTIRE tree exactly twice: that declaration and that `add`. Nothing removes from it, nothing iterates it, nothing closes it - not the host's own `close()` (`:1263-1290`, which drains `generations` and the two cleanup callbacks but not this list) and not any caller. So every process observation permanently adds one lease whose payment is never released. That is exactly the shape E197 measured: a `ps` observation pays its own phase, per-daemon snapshots and `daemons.size*4+8` more, leaks that charge, and the NEXT invocation - the `wait` - then cannot complete because the shared bank is holding those leaked payments; without a preceding `ps` nothing is leaked and the wait succeeds. This is a genuine unbounded-retention bug of the same family as the capacity/ownership items, provable by reading the file, and the first candidate in this investigation where the defect is in the code rather than in a measurement schedule. It is NOT yet proven to be the sole cause, and its correct close point (per-invocation release vs. release at host close vs. a bounded retention window) is a design decision, so it is recorded with its evidence and left unpatched. | `agent_host_runtime/src/javascript_plugin_host.cj:74,82-89,1263-1290`; `grep -rn retainedProcessServices180` returns only those two sites; E197 A/B (ps -> hang, no ps -> OK) |

| E199 | The retained-lease leak is PROVEN by measurement, not just by reading the code | A probe on `registerActualProcessLease180` printing `retainedProcessServices180.size` after each append reports **`retained process leases=5`** at the end of the E197 sequence: exactly one per read-family invocation (ps, logs, describe, send, and the wait attempt - all five are process observations), with no code path ever removing them. Each of those five invocations is a separate short-lived App process that paid its lease and then exited, so the payment is retained for the life of that process and released only by exit, while the SHARED broker daemon and its state root persist across all five - which is precisely why the later `wait` finds the bank already charged and reports nothing. So E198's reading is confirmed: `retainedProcessServices180` is an unbounded, never-drained retention (declared `:74`, appended `:82-89`, referenced nowhere else in the tree). The defect is now established with three independent lines of evidence - the code (`grep` finds only declare+add), the A/B (E197: `ps` -> hang, no `ps` -> success), and the counter (5 retained for 5 observations). Its correct close point remains a design decision (release per invocation, at host close, or within a bounded window), which is why it is recorded and not patched here. Source reverted byte-identical, probe absent from the rebuilt binary, App back to the frozen `a575949a8e4f9bd1f0da31ef803bd7daf4b9fbfaf0c471807c10a7e7e03d0024`. | probe `/tmp/probe-lease-retained.txt` = `retained process leases=5`; `diff` clean vs `/tmp/jph.bak`; App sha == `a575949a...`; `javascript_plugin_host.cj:74,82-89` |

| E200 | The `plugin.package_tree_limit` I surfaced was MY OWN environment error, and it highlighted a real trap in the runner | Two corrections in one. (a) The deterministic `workflow (goal) component import failed: plugin.package_tree_limit` I measured three times was not a product finding: `/tmp/run-ppc.sh` line 32 writes `: "${AXYNDRA_WORKFLOW_PLUGIN_PACKAGE:=$AXYNDRA_WORKFLOW_PLUGIN_PACKAGE}"`, which sets the variable to EMPTY when it is not already exported, so my repeat runs imported an empty path and the registry rejected it. Exporting the package explicitly restores the contract's real state (`second fixture request completes: context.read_failed ...`), which is the boundary E164 recorded. So E198/E199's leak finding is unaffected, but any measurement taken through that runner without exporting the workflow package is invalid - and several of my earlier repeated-run measurements predate this discovery, so they must be re-read with that in mind. (b) The empty-path rejection surfaces as `plugin.package_tree_limit`, which is misleading: a missing or empty package path is an input error and should not be reported as a tree-limit violation. Both are recorded rather than patched: the runner is my own scratch harness (not repo code) and the error-code choice belongs to the package loader's design. The surfaced-message edit was reverted byte-identical and the contract is back to HEAD. | `/tmp/run-ppc.sh:32` (`:=` empty-default); probe/`E199` unaffected; `installed_context.cj` `diff` clean vs `/tmp/ic2.bak`; real boundary reproduced only after explicit export |

| E201 | `product_prompt_contract`'s second-request blocker is unmasked: `plugin.document_grant_changed`, i.e. the operator document grant's bytes change between preparation and use | A file-write probe on the core deny path (`core.cj` `owner.deny("context.read_failed")`) captured the real result the context read returned: `{"error":"plugin.document_grant_changed","error_kind":"permission_denied","message":"complete operator document grant bytes changed during transfer"}` - not a bank or a scheduling problem at all. The message is raised at `agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_document_configuration.cj:85`, where a zero-compare of `source` against `expected` over the full length of the operator document grant fails; the sibling checks at `:92` (`loadHostPluginDocumentConfiguration` -> `current.encoded != encoded || current.witness != witness`, "operator document rights changed after preparation") and `:122` ("document grant changed during capture") test the same condition on other paths. So the second request's context read is rejected because the document grant the request prepared against is no longer byte-identical to the operator configuration at use time. The contract's own fixture is the prime suspect rather than the product: `promptCreateGoal` runs for the SECOND session and writes `plugins/documents.json` again (E133 made it reuse the installed identity, but the file write itself is repeated), so if that second write changes the grant bytes between the first request's preparation and the second request's use, this check fires exactly as observed - and it would be deterministic, which matches the 3/3 repetition. Confirming which side changed the bytes (contract fixture vs. product write) is the next step; both probes were reverted byte-identical. | core-deny probe `/tmp/probe-core-deny.txt` (full error object); `agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_document_configuration.cj:85,92,122`; `3/3` deterministic runs with the package exported (`/tmp/ppc2-{1,2,3}.log`) |

| E202 | The grant-changed cause is a rewrite-witness mismatch: the check compares nanosecond mtime/ctime, so re-writing identical bytes still fails it | Read the witness encoder: `p4_read_view_witness` (`libs/process4cj/native/process4cj_native.c:2254-2268`) renders a regular file as `f:<dev>:<ino>:<mode>:<size>:<mtime_sec>:<mtime_nsec>:<ctime_sec>:<ctime_nsec>`. `HostPluginDocumentConfiguration.validateTransferWitness` (`agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_document_configuration.cj:71-86`) compares that string for `plugins/documents.json` against the witness captured at preparation and reports `plugin.document_grant_changed` on any difference. Therefore a write that leaves the file's CONTENT byte-identical still changes the witness, because `st_mtim`/`st_ctim` advance to nanosecond resolution. The contract does exactly that: `promptCreateGoal` runs once per session and calls `promptAppendPluginDocuments` (`support_tests/product_prompt_contract/src/installed_context.cj:175-185`), which rewrites `plugins/documents.json` even when the merged entry list is unchanged - so the second session's write bumps the witness between the first request's preparation and the second request's use, and the second context read is correctly refused. The defect is in the contract's fixture, not in the product: the product's check is doing its job (it must detect a grant that changed under it), and the fixture is mutating an operator-owned file it should treat as already-configured. The minimal fix is to make `promptAppendPluginDocuments` idempotent-by-content - skip the write when the resulting document equals what is already on disk - which preserves every assertion while removing the spurious mutation. Recorded with the mechanism proven and left unpatched pending that small fixture change. | `process4cj_native.c:2254-2268` (witness includes mtime/ctime nsec); `plugin_document_configuration.cj:71-86`; `installed_context.cj:175-185`; core-deny probe output (E201) |

| E203 | The diagnosis-derived fixture fix was tried, changed the symptom, and was reverted: making the append idempotent turns the clean failure into a hang | E202 predicted that skipping the no-op rewrite of `plugins/documents.json` would clear `plugin.document_grant_changed`. It was implemented (`promptAppendPluginDocuments` now returns without writing when the merged document already equals the file) and measured twice with 900s and 1500s head-room: the contract no longer reports `second fixture request completes: context.read_failed` at all, and instead produces NO output and never returns. So the witness mismatch was indeed downstream of that rewrite - removing it changes behaviour - but the second request then blocks somewhere else entirely, which is a different and worse failure than the one it replaced. Reverted byte-identical; the baseline is confirmed unchanged (`exit=1`, `context.read_failed`, reproduced after the revert). This is recorded as a FALSIFIED fix rather than a step forward, and it establishes two things for the next attempt: the grant rewrite is genuinely part of the causal chain, and whatever it was masking is itself an unbounded wait - so the productive next instrument is a file probe on the second request's read path with the idempotent append in place, not another blind fixture change. | fix applied and reverted (`installed_context.cj` `diff` clean vs `/tmp/ic3.bak`); with fix: `ppc-fix3.log` 0 bytes / no return at 1500s; baseline after revert: `exit=1` + `context.read_failed` (`ppc-base.log`) |

| E204 | Binary hygiene: reverting probe SOURCE is not enough, the built artifact must be rebuilt and re-hashed | After reverting the E201 context-read and core-deny probes at the source level, the already-built `target/release/bin/agent_app` still contained both probe strings (`grep -ac probe-ctxread` = 1, `probe-core-deny` = 1) and its hash had drifted off the frozen value. Fixed by rebuilding: both strings are now absent and the binary is back to the frozen `a575949a8e4f9bd1f0da31ef803bd7daf4f9baf0c471807c10a7e7e03d0024`, matching `app-dso-freeze2/dual-hash.txt`. Recorded because this is the third variant of the same class of self-inflicted hazard in this session (E155/E184 were source-level reversions that destroyed or preserved inherited work; this is the artifact-level twin) and because the check is cheap: `grep -ac <probe-string> <binary>` plus a hash comparison against the frozen pair belongs after every probe teardown. | `grep -ac` on the binary before rebuild (1, 1) and after (0, 0); `sha256sum` == `a575949a8e4f9bd1f0da31ef803bd7daf4f9baf0c471807c10a7e7e03d0024` |

| E205 | Per-class isolation (the instrument E158 called for) separates contamination from real defects, and its two genuine failures are an UNREACHABLE test expectation, not a production defect | Running three suspect classes in isolation: `ProcessChannelFactoryTests` gives **PASSED 10, FAILED 2, ERROR 0** - so those two failures are real and independent of the single-process bank contamination E158 identified - while `ModelChannelLifetimeTests` and `ModelResourceCleanupTests` give `PASSED 0/1` with `ERROR 4` each, i.e. their aggregate errors do not reproduce in isolation. Since the fixture cancels through `port.cancel` -> `owner.cancellation.cancel()` with no explicit reason, the token error is always `run.cancelled`, so the assertion's expected code is UNREACHABLE by construction. The defect is therefore in the new draft TEST's expectation (both files were added by the draft commit - E206), and the correct repair is to assert the real code or pass an explicit cancellation reason, NOT to change the two production sites that agree with each other. | E223 | CORRECTION of E197-E199: `retainedProcessServices180` is DELIBERATE permanent retention, not a leak - and that closes the todo's open question | E197-E199 recorded the list as a leak of one paid lease per process observation because it is only ever `add`ed (`javascript_plugin_host.cj:86`) and never removed. Following the question the todo actually posed - is the caller supposed to close that lease? - the answer is that it CANNOT, and the source says so explicitly: `HostHubDerivedProcessLease180` carries `// No public close/refund: an unknown physical launch, scheduler alias or escaping raw resource result remains paid in the actual record. Explicit resource retirement must prove and clear every real alias first.` (`hub_derived_process_lease180.cj:607-609`), and the class exposes no public `close` (the only `close()` at `:615` belongs to the separate `HostHubOriginCurrentWindow180`). So retaining the lease object is the ONLY thing that keeps its payment alive once `task_product.cj:4507/4536` drops its local, which means the list is the retention ROOT, not dead storage, and removing the `add` would refund a payment the design forbids refunding. The observation stands (one retained lease per call, E199's probe counted 5); the characterisation was wrong - it is retention by design, with no drain because draining would violate the stated no-refund policy. Consequence for the open hang: this mechanism is now ELIMINATED as a cause, so process14's cross-invocation stall remains unexplained rather than explained by this. | `hub_derived_process_lease180.cj:607-615` (no-refund comment, no lease `close`); `javascript_plugin_host.cj:74,86`; `plugin_child_service.cj:168,220`; `task_product.cj:4507,4536`; E197-E199 |

| E224 | The same retention policy appears in the configuration layer, where it makes a predicate permanently false | `plugin_primitive_configuration.cj:147` holds a second write-only registry, `processProjections180`, filled at `:156` by `retainProcessProjection180()` (which is the shared per-configuration bank `meter` used at `plugin_child_service.cj:132,184`). Unlike the host list, this one IS read - by `canRetireOwnedData191()` at `:61`, which is `copiedPlans.isEmpty() && processProjections180.isEmpty() && ...`. Because the registry only grows, `canRetireOwnedData191()` returns false permanently after a single process projection is retained, so the configuration's owned data can never be retired once it has paid for one process lease. That is coherent with the same no-refund policy E223 found, but it is worth stating plainly as a behaviour: the predicate is not a liveness check that eventually succeeds, it is a latch. Whether that latch is intended was not decided here - it is consistency evidence for the policy, not proof of intent, and I did not change production code on that basis. | `plugin_primitive_configuration.cj:56-62,147,153-156`; `plugin_child_service.cj:132,184`; E223 |

| E225 | The authorized aggregate change silently broke the workflow document protocol's capacity rejection; A/B-proven, then fixed by pinning the pure-provider pool | The unit gate reached the workflow fixture and failed `test_typed_shared_capacity_rejection_recovers_same_actual_worker` (`AssertionError: True is not false`) - a pre-existing test at base that asserts a legal-but-oversized begin is REJECTED with `document.data_capacity`. | E227 | DECISION REVERSED: the aggregate default is back to 128 MiB, and the real fix is that each process-wide consumer declares its own capacity | E225 proved a bare `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget()` silently inherits the default, and E226 showed that raising the default made ~41 saturation assertions across nine packages unsatisfiable. | E231 | E230's relocation is REFUTED by the gate: the App bank's 128 MiB aggregate is a contract, not a shortfall - widened, it broke four packages and fixed nothing | E230 declared 512 MiB on the App's own bank because `goal_contract` needs that headroom. So the next step is to identify WHICH of those fires, by rethrowing inside `completeScope159` likewise. Masks restored identically afterwards (`diff` clean). | Rethrown run: `context.read_continuation_invalid` at `main.cj:99` vs the masked `context.query_invalid` (E230); `tool_runtime/src/context_read_preparation.cj:63-71`; `agent_core/src/context_read_update.cj:92-111`; E231 for the refuted relocation |

| E233 | The full masking chain behind both `goal_contract` and the unit gate's blocker is now mapped, and both funnel into the same discarded cause | Rethrowing at the inner mask showed `goal_contract` reproducing E229's blocker: the canonical command tool returns `tool.preparation_not_executed` (`plugin.command_tool_failed`). The chain, each stage verified by reading source: `ToolPreparationRead.complete` -> `completeScope159` (`tool_runtime/src/context_read_preparation.cj:63-71`) converts any continuation exception into `context.read_continuation_invalid`; the continuation is built at `agent_core/src/context_read_update.cj:92-97` with a boundary of `validateCurrent` -> `validateRetainedFacts` -> `validateBoundary`; `agent_host_runtime/src/direct_preparation_flow.cj:214-224` then turns a context-read rejection into `rejectParent(cause)`, and `rejectParent` (`:249-264`) substitutes `rejectedDirectPreparationOutput()` - the literal `tool.preparation_not_executed` at `agent_runtime/src/direct_preparation_rejection.cj:16` - while keeping only `cause.kind` for the Cancelled case. So the specific context-read reason is destroyed twice before either caller sees it: the exemption `if (cause.kind == Cancelled) { Err(cause) } else { execution }` is exactly where a non-Cancelled cause becomes anonymous. This explains, without further speculation, why E229 and E232 both reported uninformative codes for what the rethrow shows are context-read-time conditions. Deciding whether `rejectParent` should preserve `cause` when it is a context-read error (or whether the continuation should not be reached at all for these two callers) is a design change to Host error reporting - not attempted here, and not to be attempted without deciding it. Masks restored identically (`diff` clean on both files). | Rethrown run: `tool.preparation_not_executed` via `plugin.command_tool_failed` at `main.cj:53`; `direct_preparation_flow.cj:214-224,249-264`; `direct_preparation_rejection.cj:16`; `context_read_preparation.cj:63-71`; `context_read_update.cj:92-111`; E229/E232 |

| E234 | The gate blocker's provenance is settled: the assertion is the DRAFT's own, and the single-process suite confirms why the gate must isolate | Ran the full `agent_product` suite in ONE process with the gate's 35-variable fixture environment: `PASSED 200, ERROR 827, FAILED 11` (`/tmp/ap-full.log`) - numerically the same contamination signature E158/E167 recorded, and the reason the gate's `run_isolated_agent_product` executes every case in its own process. Provenance of the specific blocker: `checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj` existed at base (170 lines) but IS modified by the draft (`git diff e53b9f65..HEAD`: it replaces fixed `134217728` bank literals with `nativeControlMetadataCapacity173c()`), and the exact assertion the gate trips - `@Expect(nativeControlMetadataUsage113()[1] < beforeAck)` after `acknowledgeAfterConsumption()`, requiring a post-ACK refund - is present IDENTICALLY at base (base lines 125-131) and in the draft. Both readings therefore remain live and I have not resolved them: either the refund chain this case asserts was always meant to fire and a draft path prevents it, or the assertion is wrong and predates the draft. What IS established is that it is not an artifact of the aggregate change (its sibling literals now read the capacity reader) and not purely an isolation artifact (E228 caught it in isolation). The maintenance refund chain IS mechanically present - `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget` decrements `charged` at `logical_json_payload.cj:84` and `retireMetadata()` closes the owner (`application_owned_failure113.cj:139-143`) - gated by `slot.isConsumed()`, which requires `publication.isAcknowledged()`. | `/tmp/ap-full.log` (`200/827/11`, matching E158/E167); `git diff e53b9f65..HEAD -- checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj`; base lines 125-131; `application_owned_failure113.cj:129-143`; `logical_json_payload.cj:84` |

| E235 | `process14`'s stall is localized to a CUMULATIVE cause: the full read sequence hangs 3/3, but start-then-wait is clean and no single read triggers it | Re-ran the original 6-invocation repro (`start`, `ps`, `logs`, `describe`, `send`, `wait`) on the current tree: `start`/`ps`/`logs`/`describe`/`send` all return normally and `wait` produces NO output until the 20s client timeout - reproduced **3/3**. Controlled variants: (a) `start` immediately followed by `wait` alone returns a complete wait result in 11.5s, so the wait path works when it is the first read; (b) keeping exactly ONE intervening read (`ps`, or `logs`, or `describe`, or `send`) and dropping the other three makes the wait COMPLETE in every case. So the trigger is neither the wait op itself nor any single preceding observation, but the ACCUMULATION of reads before it - which is consistent with E197-E199's finding that each process observation permanently retains a paid lease (`retainedProcessServices180` grows one per call, E199 counted 5 for five read-family calls) rather than with any per-request protocol defect. Note the two things this does NOT establish: it does not show that the retained leases CAUSE the stall (the correlation is now strong but the mechanism is still unproven), and it does not show WHERE the wait blocks - the earlier probes established it is inside the client call, not in the native wait loop (E182) or the broker round trip (E213). A discriminating experiment is available and cheap: if retention is the cause, the stall should move with the number of preceding reads rather than with which op is last, which the current single-read variants cannot separate from per-op cost. | `b2b.sh` full sequence: `wait:` empty 3/3; `start`+`wait` only: complete result in 11.5s; each single-read variant: complete; E197-E199 (`retained process leases=5`), E182, E213 |

| E236 | CORRECTION of E235: the stall is FLAKY, not a deterministic accumulation, and my E235 single-sample reasoning was wrong | E235 concluded from one sample each that the stall is a cumulative read count (full sequence hung 3/3, two reads clean). Wider sampling falsifies the deterministic reading. Measured counts (1 = wait returned, 0 = hung past the 20s client timeout): full 6-invocation sequence **hang 4/4** (0,0,0,0); three-read composition `ps+logs+describe`+wait **flaky 2 hang / 2 ok** (1,1,0,0); and in an earlier pair, composition B hung 1 of 2. Crucially E235's own discriminating variant - the SAME composition that hung in E235's single sample - passed 2/2 on repeat, which is the direct falsification. So the corrected statement is: the full sequence hangs reliably, compositions at or below three reads are unreliable, and the trigger is NOT a simple count. E235's `r2`/`r3` 'dose-response' and the observe-count-vs-total-count comparison were both single-sample and are withdrawn. What survives from E235: the stall is real, occurs INSIDE the `wait` client call, and the full sequence reproduces it 4/4. What this means for the retained-lease hypothesis (E197-E199): a cumulative-resource explanation predicts monotone dependence on the number of prior reads, and this measurement is not monotone - so that hypothesis is WEAKENED but not eliminated, since retention is real and the full sequence does maximize retained leases. Reporting the sample sizes explicitly because E189/E190 taught the same lesson twice and I repeated the error. | Tally `FULL 0/4` hang, `R3 2 hang/2 ok`, `B 1 hang/1 ok`; E235 withdrawn in its dose-response part; E189/E190 (single-sample rule) |

| E237 | Session state after the capacity rework and the process-stall localization | Consolidated current state for continuation. CAPACITY: the library default is 128 MiB (`logical_json_payload.cj:32`) with the design rationale recorded in-code; no consumer was given a wider bank because E231 proved widening the App bank breaks eleven `agent_host_runtime` pressure cases; the E218/E221/E225 test edits are SEMANTICALLY VALID but INERT under the restored default (explicit-default or capacity-derived assertions), retained to avoid re-churning the memory digests. E232/E233 resolved the `goal_contract` conflict by OBSERVATION: its failure is a context-read condition (`context.read_continuation_invalid`, then `tool.preparation_not_executed`), NOT capacity, and the reason is destroyed twice before either caller sees it (`completeScope159` then `rejectParent` at `direct_preparation_flow.cj:249-264`). UNIT GATE: 16/16 fixtures and 27/27 packages run; 26 PASS; the single blocker is the draft's own `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests` refund-after-ack assertion (`checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:135`, present identically at base, recorded failing pre-session in E114). PROCESS STALL: real, inside the `wait` client call, full sequence hangs 4/4, and E236 falsified the deterministic-accumulation reading. EXTERNAL: the Material 25 cohort artifact is absent from this tree (E180), and `agent_sdk_gate.sh`/`package_readiness_gate.sh` need network. Ledger E79-E237. | this entry; E231/E232/E233/E234/E236 for each claim |

| E238 | Incidental observation from cleanup: every `hub_process_read`/`hub_process` invocation left a live broker worker behind, and they outlived the client | Cleaning up after the E235/E236 repro left 208 live `agent_app __process-broker-worker` processes and 48 daemon bash loops, ALL rooted under my `the repro scratch roots` scratch directories (verified: zero non-matching), so I killed precisely those. Two facts are worth keeping. (a) Each invocation that observed or spawned a process left its own broker worker running after the CLI client exited, which is a second form of the same non-release E197-E199 found: the retained `HostHubDerivedProcessLease180` and its worker outlive the request that created them. (b) A hung `wait` does not stop the broker: the workers were alive and healthy while the client sat at its timeout, so the stall is on the client's side of the round trip, consistent with E182/E213 having excluded the native wait loop and the broker protocol. Neither fact is a new defect claim - (a) is expected if the lease is deliberately retained (E223) and (b) is a constraint, not a cause - but together they say the stall is NOT the broker failing to answer. | `pgrep -af` listing before cleanup (208 broker workers under `the repro scratch roots`, 48 daemons); E197-E199/E223 for retention; E182/E213 for exclusions |

| E239 | During the hang the client is blocked on an in-process futex, not on the broker or any I/O | Sampled the real CLI process during the hung `wait` (identified by `/proc/<pid>/exe` pointing at `target/release/bin/agent_app` and the cmdline naming the `hub_process_read` wait invocation, because `pgrep -f` otherwise matches the `timeout` wrapper). While hung: **7 threads**, four+ in `futex_do_wait`, one in `do_epoll_wait`, and exactly **1 socket fd** open; the process is `S` and stays that way across samples. Two conclusions follow. (a) The stall is a LOCK or CONDITION wait inside the client process, not a network read: a client blocked on an unanswered broker reply would sit in `read`/`poll` on the socket (and the broker's own threads confirmed it answers, E238), whereas here the main thread waits on a futex while the epoll thread is idle. (b) Since a healthy `wait` returns in seconds and uses the same single socket, the difference between the passing and hanging runs is inside the process - which fits the observation that the hang is flaky in the composition of prior calls rather than in any single request (E236). This is a constraint that rules out the remaining I/O-shaped explanations; it does not yet name the lock. The cheap next probe is to find which mutex by looking for a worker thread holding it, since a lock held by a thread that never returns is the shape a retained/never-released owner would produce. | `/proc/<pid>/task/*/wchan` = `futex_do_wait` x6, `do_epoll_wait` x1; `ls -l /proc/<pid>/fd | grep -c socket` = 1; E236/E238 |

| E240 | Under the debugger the same `wait` does NOT hang - it fails fast with `owner.peer_disconnected`, which relocates the stall to a timing/ordering window | E239 left the hang as an in-process futex wait. Running the identical sequence with the `wait` invocation launched under gdb gave a DIFFERENT outcome: instead of blocking, the client reported `owner peer ended before the next complete frame (owner.peer_disconnected)` and **exited 1 within seconds** with all seven threads cleanly exited. Two readings, and the evidence favours the second. (a) Debugger effect: gdb stops threads at entry, changing scheduling enough to avoid the window - possible, since gdb was started before `run` rather than attached to an already-hung process. (b) More likely: the stall is a race whose two outcomes are mutually exclusive - either the client waits on a futex for a peer frame that never arrives (E239's observation) or it observes the peer as gone and reports `owner.peer_disconnected`. Under gdb (slower startup, detached-after-fork traces) the peer's exit is noticed BEFORE the client enters the wait, so it fails fast; without gdb the client enters the wait first and blocks forever because no reconnect or second frame ever arrives. That makes `owner.peer_disconnected` the proper diagnosis of the stalled case as well: the wait is blocked waiting for a frame from a worker whose peer has already ended, and nothing converts that into an error on the blocking path. It also explains E236's flakiness without any count threshold - the race lands either way depending on when the broker worker exits relative to the client's wait entry. Not yet verified: whether the worker reliably ends at that point, and where the blocking path should surface the disconnect. | gdb run: `owner peer ended before the next complete frame (owner.peer_disconnected)`, `Inferior 1 exited with code 01` in ~60s total; E239 (`futex_do_wait`) and E236 (flaky) |

| E241 | Strongest mechanism candidate for the stall: the peer frame reader retries an idle socket timeout forever | Reading `readWholeFrameHelper180` (`agent_product/src/product_owner_peer180.cj:96-124`) gives a concrete way to block indefinitely, which is what E239 observed and E240's gdb run bypassed. On `SocketTimeoutException` the reader does: `let continueIdle = idle && !closing && bytes.isEmpty()` then **`if (continueIdle) { continue }`** - i.e. as long as the peer is marked `idle`, is not closing, and no partial frame has arrived, a read timeout does not produce `owner.peer_frame_timeout` but silently retries. The only other exit is `count <= 0`, which returns `owner.peer_disconnected` (':116-117', the exact error E240 saw under gdb), or a `SocketException` that is rethrown unless `closing && bytes.isEmpty()`. So the two observed outcomes are both explained by this one function: if the read eventually returns EOF the caller gets `owner.peer_disconnected` promptly (gdb timing), and if it keeps timing out while `idle` stays true the loop never terminates (the un-gdb hang). Two qualifications I will not overstate: the E239 sample put the MAIN thread in `futex_do_wait` rather than in a socket read, so this loop may run on another thread while the main one waits on a lock/'condition' it will never receive, and I have not yet proven that `idle` remains true in the hanging case. The minimal discriminating test is to log `(idle, closing, bytes.isEmpty())` at each timeout, or to check whether forcing `idle=false` converts the hang into `owner.peer_frame_timeout`; both are cheap and neither is done. Recording it as a candidate, not a diagnosis. | `product_owner_peer180.cj:96-124` (`continueIdle` continue at :101-103, `owner.peer_disconnected` returns at :110-117); E239/E240 |

| E242 | The idle-retry is one-way and by design, which moves the defect question to the client's own bound | `idle` is a latch: declared `false` (`product_owner_peer180.cj:35`) and set `true` only by `enableAuthenticatedIdle180()` (`:55`), called once after a successful frontend handshake at `process_broker.cj:422` and once at `:2268`; nothing anywhere sets it back to false. So once a peer is authenticated it is permanently 'idle-eligible', and `readWholeFrameHelper180`'s `if (continueIdle) { continue }` (`:99-103`) means the broker side will keep re-reading rather than fail - which is defensible for a persistent authenticated session, and is therefore probably NOT the bug on its own. What that reframes is where the missing bound lives: the client's `wait` op carries an explicit user-facing `timeout` (3s in my repro) and must return within it, yet E239 observed the client parked on a futex with everything else healthy. So the open question is no longer 'why does the frame reader retry' (answered: by design) but 'which client-side wait ignores its own timeout', and the E240 gdb run shows that when the peer's end IS observed the client exits promptly with `owner.peer_disconnected` - i.e. the error path works, it is simply never taken while the session stays idle-eligible. This narrows the search to the client's wait loop and is the most specific statement this session has produced about the stall. | `product_owner_peer180.cj:35,55,99-103`; `process_broker.cj:422,2268`; E239/E240/E241 |

| E243 | Gate blocker mechanism located: the post-ACK refund requires `transferred`, which only `finishHandoff(true)` sets - so the assertion fails exactly when the consumer was acknowledged without the poll having transferred | E229 recorded that the gate's only remaining failure is `@Expect(nativeControlMetadataUsage113()[1] < beforeAck)` after `acknowledgeAfterConsumption()` (`checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:135`). Tracing the release gate end to end gives the condition. The refund path is `collectOwnedCheckpointFailure113` -> `collectOwnedFailureAcknowledgements113` -> `if (slot.isConsumed()) { slot.retireMetadata() }` (`application.cj:2651-2668`), and `retireMetadata()` is the only thing that closes the owner (`application_owned_failure113.cj:139-143`). `isConsumed()` requires THREE conditions (`:129-136`): `transferred && !invoking && !handingOff`, plus the published value being acknowledged. `transferred` is set in exactly one place - `finishHandoff(accepted)` with `accepted == true` (`:125-127`) - and `finishHandoff` is called from `admitOwnedCheckpointFailure113` (`application.cj:2515-2533`) with whatever the caller's `accept` callback returned, and from the poll paths at `:2582-2583`/`:2611-2612` where it is hardcoded true. So the assertion fails iff the slot's value was acknowledged while `transferred` stayed false, i.e. the consumer was acknowledged on a path that did not go through a poll that transferred it. That is a coherent, specific condition rather than a mystery, and it also explains why the base and draft versions behave identically (E234): the machinery is unchanged. What is NOT decided is whether the test's sequence should have transferred the slot (test error) or whether the acknowledge-after-consumption path must also mark the handoff accepted (production gap) - both readings remain open, both need an owner decision, and E203 warns against guessing. | `application.cj:2515-2533,2649-2668`; `application_owned_failure113.cj:125-143`; `checkpoint_maintenance_failure173c_test.cj:135`; E229/E234 |

| E244 | Release-gate coverage measured directly: the offline-runnable steps pass, and the two that fail do so for environment reasons with the exact errors recorded | Rather than inferring from the gate script, ran its individual steps. PASS offline: `docs_gate.py`, `tracked_artifact_gate.py`, `architecture_gate.sh`, and `tui_visual_showcase_test.py` (`OK`). With a real binary and the same `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` the gate needs (without it the candidate cannot even load: `libstdx.crypto.digest.so: cannot open shared object file`), `setup_blackbox` PASSES against `target/release/bin/agent_app`. Two steps fail for identifiable environment reasons, NOT product reasons: `package_candidate_clean_env` aborts with `FileNotFoundError: .../relocated/package-provenance.json` - it requires a PACKAGED candidate in a relocation directory, and I handed it a plain build-tree binary; and `entry_modes_blackbox` aborts with `process broker Unix socket path exceeds the portable limit (hub.process_broker_socket_path_too_long)`, which is the product correctly refusing an over-long socket path under `/tmp/axyndra-...` rather than a defect. So the honest coverage statement is: 5 release-gate steps verified green on this tree, 2 fail only for harness prerequisites I can supply (a packaged candidate) or a shorter temp path, and the rest (`product_unit_gate` 26/27 with its single known blocker, `vnext_contract_gate`, `package_readiness_gate`, `mcp_conformance` PASS per E174/E176, the contract loop, and the TUI/cold-start/profile steps) are either already measured elsewhere in this ledger or blocked on the SDK/network. Recorded because 'the release gate passes' would be an overclaim from any subset of these. | `setup_blackbox` PASS (with `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` from the SDK); `docs_gate`/`tracked_artifact_gate`/`architecture_gate`/`tui_visual_showcase_test` PASS; the two failures' verbatim errors above; E174/E176/E228 for the others |

| E245 | The two TUI gate failures are a real portability limit, not a harness artifact, and the arithmetic confirms it | `entry_modes_blackbox` and both TUI gates abort with `hub.process_broker_socket_path_too_long`, raised by `prepareBrokerRuntime` when `paths.socketPath.size > 100` (`process_broker.cj:1005-1008`); the path is `runtimeRoot + "/broker.sock"` (`:40`). Substituting a short candidate path via a symlink did NOT help, which initially looked like the check ignored my path - the correct reading is that the socket path derives from the RUNTIME ROOT (a per-session directory under the home/state root the harness chooses), so what matters is that harness-provided root's length, not the candidate's. Under this checkout the roots land under `$HOME/.cache/agent-tmp/axyndra-pr49.R8V8Px/...`, which is long enough to exceed 100 characters before `/broker.sock` is appended. So the gate's TUI steps cannot pass from a deep checkout path regardless of product correctness - a genuine portability constraint of the 100-character bound interacting with deep working directories, and the reason `release_gate.sh` runs from `$root` rather than an arbitrary temp tree. Two consequences: (a) my earlier read of this as 'the product correctly refusing an over-long path' was right about the mechanism but incomplete about the cause - the path is long because of the CHECKOUT, not because the harness chose badly; (b) to run these steps the tree must be reachable by a short path, which is testable by re-running from a short-path clone rather than by changing any code. | `process_broker.cj:31,40,1005-1008`; `entry_modes_blackbox`/`tui_golden_gate`/`tui_performance_gate` errors; E244 |

| E246 | E231's conflict is a real incompatibility, now shown to be one of SATURATION METHOD rather than of capacity - which points at the actual fix | Established by reading the reservation paths, not by inference: the host pressure tests that regressed when I widened the App bank use the VERY SAME bank (`reserveNativeDocumentPayload` -> `nativePayloadOwnerBudget.acquire(...)`, `native_payload.cj:210-213`, and `nativeControlMetadataUsage113` is literally `nativePayloadOwnerBudget.usage()`, `:40-43`), so widening it necessarily changed what they observe. The decisive detail is HOW they saturate: `LiveSessionFixture` pressure is one bounded reservation, `reserveNativeDocumentPayload(8388608)` (`host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj:74`), which `acquire` charges as `max(16 MiB, 8 * bytes)` = 16 MiB - a SINGLE owner. Their cases then assert a bounded refusal that depends on the bank's total being near its ceiling. So the host tests are not asserting '32 owners cannot coexist' (they cannot, for any aggregate, because `acquire` refuses on `count >= maximumTransfers`); they are asserting actions against a bank with limited REMAINING headroom, and a larger aggregate simply gives them headroom they did not expect. That makes the two readings compatible in principle and names the fix precisely: what these cases need is the bank to be at a known level, which is exactly what the helper style ('pin maximumCharged, then saturate') provides, rather than a global default chosen for one contract. I have NOT re-done those eleven cases on that basis - each would need the saturation re-expressed against a pinned aggregate, and E203 is the standing warning against guessing at such edits. Recorded as the concrete, bounded next step with the evidence that makes it plausible. | `native_payload.cj:35-43,210-213`; `host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj:73-95`; `logical_json_payload.cj:48-54` (count bound + max(16 MiB, 8x) charge); E231/E245 |

| E247 | Measured: at CURRENT state `goal_contract` fails, and the widened-bank workaround is confirmed incompatible with the host suite - so this is a real, undecided design question, not a missing patch | Two measurements pin the state. (1) With the bank bare and the default at 128 MiB (the state I restored in E231 for the unit gate), `goal_contract` FAILS: `tool.preparation_not_executed` at `main.cj:99`, which the mask chain (E233) reduces to a context-read cause. (2) In E231 the same contract PASSED when I declared 512 MiB on the App bank, but the unit gate then fell from 26/27 to 23/27 because eleven `agent_host_runtime` cases and three others regressed - and E246 showed why: those cases saturate the SAME bank via `reserveNativeDocumentPayload` and assert bounded refusals against its remaining headroom. So the two required observables - the contract's full-session workload and the host suite's saturation contracts - currently cannot both hold at any single value of this bank, and I did not find a value that satisfies both. E246 also names the shape of the fix (re-express the host saturation against a pinned aggregate via the helper style already used at E218/E221/E225), but executing that is a 15-case edit across four packages whose correct per-case saturation level has to be derived case by case - that is a maintainer-level call about which contract is authoritative for this bank, and the repository's own rules say such conditional items must not be decided unilaterally. Handled by recording rather than guessing, because E203 is a live example of a plausible-looking fix that made the symptom worse. | `goal_contract` run at current state: `tool.preparation_not_executed` at `main.cj:99`; E231's gate evidence (23/27 with 512 MiB); E246 for the shared-bank mechanism |

| E248 | E246's proposed fix is NOT mechanical: these host cases use a headroom ORACLE calibrated to 128 MiB, so re-expressing them is a redesign | Reading one regressed case exactly (`host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj:73-96`) shows the mechanism. `liveSessionProbe()` (`:37-39`) reserves 4 MiB - charged `max(16 MiB, 8x)` = 32 MiB - and returns true only if that SUCCEEDS, i.e. it is a 'is there >= 32 MiB headroom?' oracle. The case then asserts a sequence of oracle readings around a retained custody payment: `true` before the session holds anything, `false` once private output is retained, `false` after releasing transient state, `true` again after close. Those truth values hold only while the aggregate is small enough that the case's own 64 MiB pressure reservation (`reserveNativeDocumentPayload(8388608)` -> `max(16 MiB, 8x)` = 64 MiB, `native_payload.cj:210-213`) plus the retained custody pushes the bank within 32 MiB of its ceiling. At 512 MiB every oracle reading becomes `true` and the `false` expectations invert. Worse for the mechanical-fix plan: `reserveNativeDocumentPayload` caps at 8 MiB, so its charge cannot exceed 64 MiB and a single case cannot cheaply fill a 512 MiB bank - filling it would take roughly eight such reservations and would change what each case is actually testing. So E246 was right about the mechanism and too optimistic about the cost: making these cases pass at a larger aggregate means redesigning each oracle's calibration, not sweeping a literal. That makes E247's conclusion firmer - the choice between (a) keeping 128 MiB and finding where `goal_contract`'s context-read failure actually comes from, and (b) moving to a larger aggregate and redesigning ~15 host cases, is a maintainer decision. | `host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj:37-39,73-96`; `native_payload.cj:210-213`; `logical_json_payload.cj:48-54`; E246/E247 |

| E249 | Two closures: the working-tree test-extension file is inherited (not mine), and `goal_contract`'s failure is serial-reproducible (not concurrency) | (1) `agent_extension_runtime/src/native_payload_test.cj` shows as ` M` in the working tree, but it is NOT from this session: its mtime is 2026-10-13 (this session's work is 2026-10-18/19) and its diff extends the native payload test's mode list with `uppercase_hex`/`invalid_hex`/`odd_hex`/`extra_hex`, which is inherited draft work unrelated to the capacity question. Recorded so it is not mistaken for an uncommitted edit of mine; I left it untouched. (2) With every probe reverted and the chain clean, `goal_contract` failed with the mask message I had installed in the PREVIOUS run, which proves the failing path is the same one and, more usefully, that the contract is REPRODUCIBLE SERIALLY - a concurrency or load artifact would not have failed identically twice in isolation. That removes the last remaining alternative explanation for `goal_contract` (E231 left open that its pass at 512 MiB might have been incidental); it is a deterministic failure of the context-read path and needs the context-read diagnosis E232/E233 described, not a capacity knob. | `git diff` of the test file and its 2026-10-13 mtime; the two identical mask-message failures; E232/E233/E247 |

| E250 | Gate run 8 reproduces 26/27 at the FINAL state, confirming the restored default is stable and the single blocker is the only remaining package failure | After the A5-03 matrix correction and all capacity reverts, re-ran `product_unit_gate.py` end to end: `plugin_fixtures 16/16`, `packages 27`, **26 PASS**, and the one FAIL is `agent_product` stopped at `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests` with `26 / 1038` (`err 0, fail 1`). This is identical to E228's result, so the state is stable across runs rather than a lucky sample - the same 26 packages pass and the same single case blocks. Two things this closes. | E251 | `goal_contract`'s inner cause is NOT reachable by source-level probing within this session, and all probes are reverted with inherited work intact | Attempted to reach the innermost cause by propagating messages at each mask in turn. So `context.query_invalid` is being produced by a site my grep did not identify as a producer - the remaining candidate is a plugin-side pure error (`plugins/*/src/*.cj` `context_query_invalid` family, per `native_context_provider.cj:25`) mapped up through the error-category table (`registry_static_error_categories78.cj:332`), which means the failure is inside the WORKFLOW PLUGIN's context provider rather than in the Host's wrapping. One of them, `agent_host_runtime/src/direct_preparation_flow.cj`, is INHERITED-dirty (its working tree adds `executionSettings180`/`modelFaultCounters180` to `directPreparationContext`, which is in neither HEAD nor the draft commit - it belongs to the inherited `model_stream_fault_counters180.cj`/`frozen_model_stream_fault180.cj` work). My backup/revert restored that inherited state rather than reverting to HEAD, which is the required behaviour; the file is ` M` in git status for that inherited reason, not because of me. | `grep PROBE` = 0 files; `diff` byte-identical on all three files; `git diff` of `direct_preparation_flow.cj` showing the inherited `executionSettings180` field; `git show HEAD:...| grep services: context.services` (no such fields); E233/E241 |

| E252 | CORRECTION of E251 and a method lesson: only error CODES survive to the reported diagnostic, so message-prefix probes are invisible - and the plugin-side hypothesis was wrong | E251 concluded the failure 'must be plugin-side' because message prefixes I injected at the Host masks did not appear. That inference was invalid, and the source says why: `CoreContextUpdate.deny(reason)` stores only `code = reason` (`context_read_update.cj:67-71`), `diagnostic()` returns that code, and `core.cj:2721-2723` reports `update.diagnostic()` as the AgentError code. So ANY probe that only changes an error MESSAGE is unreachable by construction - including all three of my earlier propagation attempts, whose silence was evidence about the mechanism, not about which layer failed. Two follow-ups also resolved: (a) instrumenting the workflow plugin's own schema gate (`plugins/workflow/src/goal_context.cj:14`, `workflow.context_query_invalid`) produced no marker either, so the plugin's goal-context schema branch is not the producer; (b) tagging the three `deny` call sites in `core.cj` with distinct CODES (`P1:`/`P2:`/`P3:`) should have identified the propagator, but the rebuilt contract reported the untagged `context.query_invalid` - and the binary check showed those `P1:`/`P2:` strings are compiler-internal (`P1:`,`P3:divmuloffsub` are Cangjie runtime tags), so that measurement was confounded and I am not treating it as evidence either way. Corrected statement: the initial-context failure's propagator is NOT yet identified; what IS known is that the code `context.query_invalid` is stored by `deny` and that message-level instrumentation cannot reach it. The remaining sound approach is to tag the deny sites' codes and verify the tag actually appears in the built binary before drawing any conclusion - a build-freshness check I skipped. All probe edits reverted: `grep` for `PROBE`/`P1:`/`P2:`/`P3:` across the tree returns 0 files. | `context_read_update.cj:67-71,176`; `core.cj:2721-2723`; `plugins/workflow/src/goal_context.cj:14`; `strings` on the contract binary showing compiler `P1:`/`P3:` tags; E251 corrected |

| E253 | BREAKTHROUGH: `goal_contract`'s real cause IS capacity, on the Host's observed content-query reservation - which resolves the E231/E247 conflict and restores E166's original reading | After E252 established that only error CODES survive, I changed the CODE at `plugin_context_provider.cj` (not the message), with a build-freshness check (`strings ... | grep -c zzcode-` = 2) so the result could not be a stale artifact. The reported diagnostic changed to `zzcode-observed`, proving the failure comes from the OBSERVED branch's constructor - `FrozenPluginContentQuery(query, selectedIds, observed: true)` at `:87`. Routing the exception's own text into the code then gave the exact cause: **`ZE:Exception: logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted`**. The arithmetic confirms it: `observed: true` selects `CONTENT_PAYLOAD_INPUT_BYTES` = `CONTENT_PAYLOAD_CONTEXT_SNAPSHOT_BYTES` (4 MiB) + 256 KiB = 4,456,448 (`content_payload_limits.cj:11-13`), and `reserveNativeContentJson` charges the bank `logicalJsonWriteReservationBytes(value, maximum)` = `min(maximum, max(1, upper))` (`logical_json_payload.cj:502-507`), i.e. UP TO the full 4.4 MiB ceiling per observed query - while `acquire` charges `max(16 MiB, 8 x bytes)`, so each observed query costs AT LEAST 16 MiB of a 128 MiB bank. Several per session exhaust it. So: E166 was RIGHT that raising the aggregate fixes `goal_contract`, E232/E233 were right that the immediate error is a context-read code, E247/E248 were right that the host suite cannot tolerate a wider bank, and E251/E252's plugin-side hypothesis was wrong. The masked message ('observed content exceeds its bounded codec or aggregate capacity') was accurate all along, and the E231-era conclusion that 'widening merely moved an unrelated check' was MY error. All probes reverted and verified (`grep` for PROBE/ZE:/zzcode = 0 files; `plugin_context_provider.cj` byte-identical). | Code-change experiment with freshness check; reported code `zzcode-observed` then `ZE:Exception: logical payload aggregate capacity is exhausted`; `plugin_context_provider.cj:80-91`; `content_payload_limits.cj:11-13`; `logical_json_payload.cj:502-507,48-54`; E166/E231/E232/E247/E252 |

| E254 | FINAL determination on the bank conflict: E248's 'not mechanical' is now proven arithmetically, and the decision is reduced to a clean two-way choice | E253 proved the App genuinely needs more than 128 MiB for a full session (each observed content query charges at least 16 MiB). That made a scaled oracle look like the reconciliation, so I checked whether the host oracle CAN be scaled - it cannot, and the arithmetic is decisive. `liveSessionProbe()` must make a reservation FAIL once the case has taken its own pressure, i.e. it must be able to consume a sizeable fraction of the bank while checking. But a single reservation is capped twice: `reserveNativeDocumentPayload` rejects `bytes > 8388608` (`native_payload.cj:211`) and `acquire` charges `max(16 MiB, 8 x bytes)` (`logical_json_payload.cj:50-51`), so the largest charge one reservation can add is **64 MiB**. Against a 128 MiB bank that is half the bank - ample to make the oracle fail as intended. Against a 512 MiB bank it is one eighth, so the oracle would report 'headroom available' and invert every assertion. Scaling the oracle would require holding several 64 MiB reservations simultaneously (8 of the 32 permitted), which changes what each case tests rather than rescaling it. So there is no mechanical reconciliation, exactly as E248 suspected and now with the bound named: the design constraint is per-reservation-max 64 MiB vs a bank size that must exceed ~128 MiB for a real session. Two clean options remain, both requiring a maintainer call: (a) keep 128 MiB (verified: unit gate 26/27, host suite green) and give the App's observed-context path a larger *dedicated* budget so a full session fits - the narrowest product fix, since only that path needs the headroom; or (b) move the bank to >=512 MiB and redesign the ~15 affected host cases, which is a test-contract change. Recording the bound because it is the fact that decides between them. | `native_payload.cj:210-213`; `logical_json_payload.cj:48-54`; `host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj:37-39`; E248/E253 |

| E255 | Both remaining options for the bank conflict violate a design invariant, which is why it needs the maintainer and not a patch | E254 reduced the choice to (a) a dedicated budget for the App's observed-context path or (b) a >=512 MiB bank with the host saturation cases redesigned. Checking each against the design shows neither is free. Option (a) breaks the unification the bank exists for - `libs/pure_provider_runtime/src/wire.cj:12-14` states 'Allocation accounting is shared by physical frames, logical input and the result consumer. It is not a capability, a Run, or a second source registry' - so giving the observed-query path its own pool would reintroduce exactly the second accounting authority that comment forbids, and `reserveNativeContentJson` is already shared with `plugin_initial_context_journal.cj:49`. Option (b) requires redesigning 8 oracle cases in `host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj` (plus the 4 other packages E231 measured), and E254 proves the oracle cannot be rescaled by arithmetic: one reservation tops out at 64 MiB (`native_payload.cj:211` caps bytes at 8 MiB, `acquire` charges `max(16 MiB, 8 x bytes)`), so against 512 MiB it reports headroom and inverts. So the honest conclusion is that this is a genuine architectural tension between 'one accounting authority' and a session that needs more than the bank's 8-owner capacity at 128 MiB (E91: the 16 MiB floor means 128 MiB admits at most eight live owners; E253: each observed content query costs at least one of them). Resolving it means changing either the accounting model or the test contracts, and both are maintainer-level. Current verified state is the conservative side of that choice: default 128 MiB, unit gate 26/27, host suite green, all six repo gates green. | `wire.cj:12-14` (unified-accounting invariant); `native_payload.cj:210-213`; `logical_json_payload.cj:50-51`; `plugin_initial_context_journal.cj:49`; `host_live_policy_session_custody_test.cj:37-39`; E91/E231/E248/E254 |

| E256 | FIX APPLIED: the observed content-query profile now has its own declared pool, which clears the capacity failure WITHOUT breaking the host oracle suite - option (a) works | E255 argued a dedicated budget would violate the unified-accounting invariant, but that objection was too strong: the repository already runs SEVEN independent pools with per-consumer capacities (`native_payload`, `pure_provider_runtime`, `model_provider_runtime` at 256 MiB, and the program/shell_readonly/skills/workspace plugin owners), so per-consumer capacity is the established pattern rather than a new authority. Implemented: `nativeObservedQueryBudget = LogicalJsonOwnerBudget(maximumCharged: 536870912)` (`native_payload.cj:47`) with `reserveNativeObservedQueryJson` (`:49-51`), and `HostFrozenPluginContentQuery`'s observed branch reroutes to it (`plugin_owner_helpers.cj:22-26`). Measured, and this is the reconciliation the session was missing: (1) `goal_contract`'s failure code CHANGED from `context.query_invalid` to `extension.protocol_failed`, i.e. the capacity failure is gone and a different (separate) issue is now exposed; (2) the host oracle suite `HostLivePolicySessionCustodyTest` passes **6/6, 0 errors, 0 failures** - the very suite whose oracle E248/E254 showed cannot tolerate a widened shared bank, because it now still sees the shared bank at 128 MiB. No test asserted that the observed query charges the shared bank (`grep` for `reserveNativeContentJson`/`HostFrozenPluginContentQuery` in `*_test.cj` = 0 hits). So the bank conflict is RESOLVED: the narrow consumer gets the headroom it needs, the shared bank keeps the value its 8-owner saturation contracts require, and the library default stays at the authorized/conservative 128 MiB. | `native_payload.cj:47-51`; `plugin_owner_helpers.cj:22-26`; `agent_host_runtime` full suite `PASSED 219, ERROR 39, FAILED 0` (identical to the pre-change baseline); `HostLivePolicySessionCustodyTest 6/6`; `goal_contract` now `extension.protocol_failed`; E248/E254/E255 |

| E257 | Gate run 9: the dedicated observed-query pool is a strict improvement - 26/27 maintained, no regression, and the capacity blocker is gone | Re-ran `product_unit_gate.py` with the E256 change: `plugin_fixtures 16/16`, `packages 27`, **26 PASS**, sole FAIL still `agent_product` at `InstalledMaintenanceFailureConsumer173cTests` (`26/1038`, 0 errors) - identical to the pre-change E228/E250 runs, so the reroute caused no regression anywhere in the tree. Combined with E256's two positive results (the host oracle suite at 6/6 and `goal_contract`'s capacity failure replaced by a different code), the change is a strict improvement: it unblocks the observed-query path without moving the shared bank that eight-owner saturation contracts depend on. The two remaining unknowns are now both bounded and quantified rather than vague: (1) the unit gate's single blocker, whose mechanism E243 locates in the `transferred` flag; (2) `goal_contract`'s newly exposed `extension.protocol_failed` ('extension invocation loop ended', `javascript_host.cj:788`), a SEPARATE defect the capacity failure had been hiding. | Gate run 9: `status FAIL`, `fixtures 16`, `packages 27`, `PASS 26`, sole FAIL `agent_product` (`26/1038`); E228/E250 identical; E243/E256 |

| E258 | The App bank had THREE starved consumers, not one: the invocation frame/result budget was the second, and giving each its own declared pool removes the capacity failure entirely | E256 gave the observed content-query profile its own pool, which moved `goal_contract`'s code from `context.query_invalid` to `extension.protocol_failed`. So the capacity problem that has framed this whole session is now RESOLVED for the goal path, and what remains there is an ordinary logic/expectation gap. | E259 | The invocation budget reroute is REFUTED and reverted: it broke 8 agent_extension_runtime cases whose CONTRACT is that frames charge the shared bank | E258 rerouted the plugin invocation's `payloadBudget` to its own 512 MiB pool to clear `goal_contract`'s `EFR:` capacity frame error. Each asserts cross-boundary GLOBAL byte retention on the shared bank, so a private invocation pool removes exactly what they measure. UNCHANGED AND DELIBERATE: the library default stays 128 MiB and no other consumer was widened. `goal_contract` now advances past all resource exhaustion and fails on a real semantic assertion, `continuation.document_observation_invalid` (`expected_revision: 54`). | E261 | Why the invocation budget must stay shared while the observed-query pool must not: the tests measure them differently, and the source distinguishes ACCUMULATION from THROUGHPUT | E259 left the two cases looking asymmetrical, so I read what each test actually measures. `content_receive_capacity209_test.cj:68-80` takes `nativePayloadOwnerBudget.usage()` BEFORE and after a `NativePayloadProcessBudget().acquire(65536)`, i.e. it requires the invocation reservation to be visible on the SHARED bank - the cross-boundary global-byte sharing is the property under test. Nothing measures `nativeObservedQueryBudget`: no test reads it and none asserts that the observed query's charge appears on the shared bank (E256's grep = 0 hits). The source explains the difference. The observed content query accumulates - each query keeps the whole content profile paid as long as its frozen DOM lives, and they accumulate across a session until the bank is gone (E253), which is precisely what a per-consumer declared capacity is for. The invocation budget is comparatively short-lived per call and its shared visibility is the contract, so moving it only removes the property the tests exist to check. So the boundary rule is now explicit and measured rather than inferred: a consumer gets its own declared pool when its exposure is ACCUMULATION that starves others, and stays on the shared bank when its cross-boundary visibility is itself asserted. That is why E256 stands and E259 was reverted, and it gives the criterion any future consumer can be judged against. | `content_receive_capacity209_test.cj:68-80`; E256 (0 grep hits for the observed pool); `native_payload.cj:47-51,70`; E253/E259 |

| E262 | CORRECTION of E256/E260: the observed-query pool does NOT resolve the bank conflict - the invocation budget needs headroom too and cannot have a private pool | E256 concluded the conflict was resolved and E260 repeated that `goal_contract` 'advances past all resource exhaustion'. So there are (at least) two capacity consumers in this path, and the second one CANNOT be given a private pool because its shared-bank visibility is what eight `agent_extension_runtime` cases assert (E259/E261). Therefore the bank conflict is NOT resolved: `goal_contract` can only pass when the shared bank has headroom for the invocation, and the host oracle cases cannot tolerate that headroom (E248/E254). The observed-query pool remains a correct, kept change - it removes one real starvation and passed the gate with no regression (E257) - but it is a partial improvement, not the resolution I claimed. Stating this plainly because E256/E260 overstated it, and the difference matters: the maintainer decision E247/E254 described is still open and still the blocker for this contract. | `goal_contract` with only `nativeObservedQueryBudget`: `extension.protocol_failed` (capacity message per E258); E259 (eight sharing tests); E248/E254 (oracle); E261 (criterion) |

| E264 | Why the `rejectParent` masking should NOT be 'fixed' without a decision: the generic code looks deliberate | E233 mapped the chain that reduces a context-read cause to `tool.preparation_not_executed` and flagged the substitution for a maintainer decision. Before implementing the obvious 'preserve the cause' change I checked whether the generic code is load-bearing, and the evidence says it is likely intentional rather than an oversight: `tool.preparation_not_executed` appears in exactly two non-test places - its definition (`agent_runtime/src/direct_preparation_rejection.cj:16`) and the WORKSPACE DIAGNOSTIC LITERAL CODE LIST (`agent_product/src/workspace_diagnostic_formatter_test.cj:7`), i.e. it is a published, enumerated diagnostic surface rather than an ad-hoc string. A Host that reports a generic 'the Direct tool did not execute because preparation did not complete' for a rejected continuation is an abstraction choice: it tells the caller the call did not run without exposing which internal context-read condition refused it. So the substitution has two defensible readings - a diagnosability defect (E233's) or a deliberate boundary (this one) - and they are not separable by reading the caller alone. Not implementing, per E233's own note and the E203 lesson. What WOULD be safe and useful is keeping the existing public code while attaching the underlying cause as a diagnostic FIELD, which preserves the published contract and restores the lost information; that is a concrete, low-risk proposal for the maintainer rather than an unattended edit. | `direct_preparation_rejection.cj:16`; `workspace_diagnostic_formatter_test.cj:7`; `direct_preparation_flow.cj:260`; E233 |

| E267 | Consolidated state after the threshold measurements, for continuation | The session's central question - what bank size this product needs - is now answered by measurement rather than argument. The bank has NO common solution: the host suite requires 128 MiB (clean) and already breaks at 192 (5 failures), while `goal_contract` requires at least 256 MiB (PASS at 256, FAIL at 192), and 192 satisfies neither (E266). Both ends are explained by mechanisms established earlier: the contract keeps several 16 MiB floor owners live for its observed content queries (E253), and the host cases assert a bounded refusal that only holds while the bank is small (E248). One real fix is KEPT and verified: the observed content-query profile has its own declared 512 MiB pool (`nativeObservedQueryBudget`, `native_payload.cj:47-51`; reroute `plugin_owner_helpers.cj:22-26`), which removed one genuine starvation with no gate regression and without disturbing the oracle (E256/E257/E263). One fix was REFUTED and reverted: the same treatment for the invocation frame/result budget, because eight cases assert its charge on the shared bank (E259/E261). The library default is restored to 128 MiB and the tree is byte-verified against its backups; `goal_contract` therefore still fails on capacity (`extension.protocol_failed` via the `Frame` arm, E258), which is the correct conservative state rather than a hidden patch. | E270 | The fill-to-capacity fix E269 proposed is NOT safely automatable: its correctness window is R-wide and R is unmeasured | Attempted to implement E269's proposal rather than leave it as an idea, and the arithmetic stopped it. The two assertions in these cases require, simultaneously, that the 32 MiB oracle fits BEFORE the output is retained and does not fit AFTER: with `C` the capacity, `P` the case's own pressure and `R` the retained output, that is `P + 32 <= C` and `P + R + 32 > C`, i.e. the permissible `P` window is `(C - 32 - R, C - 32]` - exactly `R` wide. Since `R` is the size of the retained decoded private output and nothing in the test or the source states it, the window cannot be computed; empirically it is wide enough at 128 MiB that the existing fixed `P = 64 MiB` works (the case's comment says exactly that), and at 256 MiB it is not. A fill-to-capacity helper would also have to solve this in 16-64 MiB charge quanta (`reserveNativeDocumentPayload` caps bytes at 8 MiB, `acquire` charges `8 x bytes`, so charges are >= 16 MiB), so obtaining a target remaining value inside an unmeasured window is guesswork rather than a mechanical conversion - and guessing here is precisely what E203 warns against. Reverted: no edit was made to the file (`diff` against backup confirms). Recorded as the terminal state of this line of work: the capacity conflict is fully characterised and deliberately unresolved, with two calibrated cases and an R-width constraint a maintainer can act on. | `context_private_output_test.cj:22-28,88-123`; `native_payload.cj:230`; `logical_json_payload.cj:47`; arithmetic above; E203/E248/E266/E269 |

| E285 | Arithmetic on E284's numbers shows the demanded peak is far larger than the case's input, so 'raise the ceiling' is not obviously the fix here | E284 measured `grow` demanding 105.2 MiB against 97 MiB of free headroom, implying a target decoded peak of `charged + wanted` = **139.9 MiB against the 128 MiB cap**, for a case whose report is **0.29 MiB** (300,000 bytes). Working backwards through the contract's own formula (`ensureDecoded`: `minimum = max(bytes * 8, bytes * 4 + nodes * 512 + 1048576)`, `nodes <= 65536`) gives the implied input sizes: 17.5 MiB if the bytes term dominates, or 26.7 MiB even with the node term maxed out - both roughly 60-90x the actual report. So either the reservation is computed against a much larger envelope than the report (a documented ceiling rather than the actual content), or this path over-reserves; and either way the case cannot pass at any ceiling below ~140 MiB regardless of the shared-bank question E266 examined. That is a DIFFERENT question from E266's, and it means the two capacity findings do not merge: E266 is about how many 16 MiB floor owners a session holds, this one is about a single decoded-growth demand exceeding the whole pool. Recorded because it changes what a maintainer should look at first for this case - the reservation's basis, not the ceiling. Not investigated further this turn: the next step is to find which caller passes those bytes/nodes, which I have not traced. | E284's probe numbers; `logical_json_payload.cj:175-181`; the case's 300,000-byte report |

| E287 | Caller trace for the oversized demand did NOT complete: four probe attempts, each stopped by a distinct tooling obstacle rather than by the question | Attempted to identify which caller makes the 105.2 MiB growth demand (E284/E285) by logging the `(bytes, nodes)` pair at `ensureDecoded`. | E288 | Static quantification of the oversized demand, and why it is a strictly harder constraint than E266's | The arithmetic from E284/E285, made explicit: the pool had `charged = 36,372,960` (34.7 MiB) against `cap = 134,217,728`, so free headroom was **93.3 MiB**, and the single `grow` asked for **105.2 MiB** - 11.9 MiB more than the entire remaining pool. CORRECTED precision: `grow`'s argument is `minimum - cost` where `cost` is **that reservation's own** charge, not the bank's `charged` (`ensureRetainedPeak`, `logical_json_payload.cj:184-198`), so the refusal condition is `grow > cap - charged` = 105.2 MiB > 93.3 MiB. The bank would have to reach `charged + grow` = **139.9 MiB** to admit it, against a 128 MiB cap. So the accurate statement is that this ONE reservation raises its own peak by 105.2 MiB on top of 34.7 MiB already held by others, and the pool therefore cannot hold both - still a strictly harder constraint than E266's many-small-owners case, but not the same as claiming one owner needs 105.2 MiB of a 128 MiB pool to itself. That has a concrete consequence for the pending decision: raising the ceiling to 256 MiB would accommodate this case, but it also means the pool is being asked to hold a single 105 MiB decoded peak - so a maintainer should first ask whether that peak is the intended cost of a 0.29 MiB report (E285's ~60-90x mismatch) or an over-reservation, because if it is an over-reservation then widening the pool merely hides it, which is the same reasoning that made E225's workflow guard a real bug rather than a capacity shortfall. Also worth recording, with the bound checked: `maximumMessage` is 16 MiB in this pool, and the formula's bytes term is `bytes * 8`, so a maximal 16 MiB input would demand `16 MiB * 8 = 134,217,728` - exactly the pool's cap. The 105.2 MiB growth is therefore consistent with a bytes term of roughly 13.8 MiB (or `bytes*4 + nodes*512` at a larger `bytes`), i.e. an input far larger than the 0.29 MiB report under test - which is what makes E285's mismatch question the right one to ask before any ceiling change. | E284/E285 probe numbers; `charged=36372960`, `cap=134217728`, `grow=110295216`; `logical_json_payload.cj:175-181` formula; `maximumMessage` 16 MiB |

| E294 | The pre-existing `agent_product` failure is localized: it stalls inside a product MODEL SUBMIT against a 3001-record memory window, which is the SAME shape as the `wait` race | Used the case's own stage diagnostic (gated behind `AXYNDRA_DIAGNOSTIC_MEMORY_WINDOW_APPLICATION=1`, which is why the default run printed nothing). | E295 | E294's hypothesis is CORROBORATED by kernel state: the stuck memory-window submit shows the identical blocking signature as the `wait` race | Sampled the real test process (identified by `/proc/<pid>/exe` = `agent_product/target/release/unittest_bin/std.testrunner`, after my first attempt sampled a `sigsuspend` shell wrapper instead - the same mistake E239 records).
