That is the same signature E239 measured for the stalled `hub_process_read` wait (7 threads, main in `futex_do_wait`, one idle in `do_epoll_wait`), in a completely different feature area - one is a process-observation wait, the other a memory-window model submit with 3001 seeded records. Two independent product entry points blocking with an identical in-process lock/wait shape is much stronger evidence for a shared root cause than E294's timing coincidence alone, and it argues against both being independent feature bugs. What this still does NOT establish: that the shared cause is one specific lock or condition variable - that requires naming the holder, which needs a debugger or a targeted probe, and E287/E289 showed how costly probing this tree is. So the claim is 'same signature, same likely class', not 'same line of code'. | `/proc/<pid>/task/*/wchan` for the stuck `std.testrunner`: `futex_do_wait` x6, `do_epoll_wait` x1, 2 sockets; E239 (identical signature for `hub_process_read`), E294 |

| E296 | One concrete candidate for the shared blocking signature, from the source rather than from another probe | E295 established that a process-observation wait and a memory-window model submit block with the identical in-process signature. Tracing what both flows have in common gives a single candidate: every plugin invocation constructs `HostJavaScriptPluginHost.payloadBudget`, which is a `NativePayloadProcessBudget` whose `inner` is `LogicalJsonProcessBudget(nativePayloadOwnerBudget)` (`native_payload.cj:64-70`), and the invocation loop also takes `nativePayloadOwnerBudget.acquireFixedBuffer(8192)` per call (`javascript_host.cj:1509`). So both stalled flows charge the SAME 128 MiB App bank on every invocation - the bank E288 measured as the pool whose `grow` was refused, and the subject of the whole capacity thread. That makes 'both stalls contend on the shared bank' a single coherent hypothesis covering all the session's capacity and stall observations at once. It is NOT verified: `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget` holds its `Mutex` only around short counter updates, so a mere charge cannot block indefinitely, and the observed block is on the MAIN thread's futex with no work in flight - so if the bank is the cause it would have to be through a caller that holds a lock across a blocked operation, not through the charge itself. I did not find that caller. Recorded as the leading hypothesis with its own refutation condition stated, rather than as a diagnosis, because the last three times I attributed a capacity-shaped symptom without measuring the specific pool (E225, E259, E283) the attribution was wrong. | `native_payload.cj:64-70`; `javascript_host.cj:439,1509`; `logical_json_payload.cj` (Mutual exclusion scope); E288/E295 |

| E297 | The stall is NOT a Cangjie scheduler or lock deadlock: every runtime thread is IDLE | Read the thread NAMES of the stuck process (`/proc/<pid>/task/<tid>/comm`), which my earlier `/proc` sampling had not done. All seven are runtime-internal and all parked: `std.testrunner` (main) `futex_do_wait`, `gc-pool-t1`/`gc-main-thread`/`gc-helper` `futex_do_wait`, `schmon` `do_epoll_wait`, and TWO `schd-worker` threads both in `futex_do_wait`. That is decisive for the shape of the defect: if a lock were held by a live worker, that worker would be RUNNING or blocked in a syscall doing work, and the scheduler would have runnable work; instead EVERY `schd-worker` is parked waiting for work, which means no Cangjie task holds anything - the case is blocked on something OUTSIDE the Cangjie thread pool. Combined with E294 (stages 1-7 done in 1.1 s, nothing after `app.submit`), the case is waiting on an external resource that never arrives: a child process, a socket, or a timer - which is exactly the class of thing E295's 2 open sockets points at. This also REFUTES the most natural reading of E296: a contention/deadlock on an in-process Cangjie lock would show a busy or syscall-blocked worker, and none exists. So the leading candidate moves from 'shared bank lock' to 'an external wait with no arrival', which is a much more actionable shape - it is the same class as E239's `wait` (client parked, broker healthy). | `/proc/<pid>/task/*/comm` + `wchan` for the stuck `std.testrunner` (all seven idle); E294/E295/E296 |

| E298 | Sampling the RIGHT process reverses the shape again: the test worker's main thread is RUNNING (state R), and the memory-plugin binary is open | E297 read the `std.testrunner` parent and concluded 'all threads idle, blocked on an external wait'. Sampling the actual worker (found via `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` containing `internalWorkerId=0`, because `std.testrunner` is only the parent that dispatches to a child worker over `--internalMainProcessPort`) shows the opposite: `comm=agent_product` on the MAIN thread with **state R and `wchan=0`** - it is not blocked at all, it is spinning or doing work - while the gc/scheduler threads sit ordinarily parked. Its fd table is also informative: an open SQLite triple (`state.db`, `-wal`, `-shm`), `/dev/pts/16` twice (a pty), and fd 14 pointing at `.../settings/plugins/packages/user/<digest>/bin/memory-plugin` - the installed memory plugin binary. So the case is not stuck waiting on a forgotten resource; the worker is actively running for the full 60 s against a 3001-record memory window with the memory plugin installed. That reframes the failure as work that does not finish in the budget rather than a lost wakeup, and it withdraws E297 rather than extending it. The lesson repeated three times now: the same tree needs the CORRECT process sampled (E239 sampled a `timeout` wrapper, E295 the idle parent, E298 finally the worker), and each earlier sample supported a wrong conclusion. | `/proc/<worker>/task/*` with `comm`+state: main `R`, wchan `0`; fds incl. `bin/memory-plugin` and a state.db triple; E294/E295/E297 withdrawn |

| E299 | The pre-existing failure is CPU-BOUND and does not finish even at 5x the budget: `app.submit` spins on a 3001-record memory window | Two measurements settle its nature. (1) Sampling the worker's CPU over time: state is `R` with `wchan=0` throughout and `utime` climbs **~380 clock ticks per 4-second sample** (42 -> 4247 across 12 samples, ~88 CPU-seconds in ~48 s wall), i.e. roughly 95% of one core for the whole budget with no progress. (2) The earlier 300-second run (E293's `/tmp/c3.log`) failed at exactly `300,000,969,455 ns`, so **5x the gate's 60 s budget also does not complete**. Together: the submit over a 3001-record memory window is effectively non-terminating at this size, not merely slow, and it burns a full core while failing to finish. That is a genuine product pathology in the memory-window/context-read path rather than a fixture or environment artifact, and it is consistent with E294 (stages 1-7, including the 3000-record insert, complete in 1.1 s - so the seeding is fine and the cost is inside the submit). What remains unidentified is WHICH loop: naming it needs either a profiler (perf was available earlier in this environment) or a call-site probe, and I have not run either, so the claim stops at 'CPU-bound, non-terminating at 3001 records'. This also finally explains the case's very specific name ('FindsRecord3001WithoutDroppingHistoricalFacts'): it is a window-scanning case exercised at the record count where the scan stops terminating, which makes the count 3001 itself the likely trigger. | `utime` series 42,415,790,1169,1552,1944,2334,2723,3103,3480,3862,4247 at 4 s intervals; `/tmp/c3.log` failure at `300,000,969,455 ns`; E293/E294/E298 |

| E300 | Naming the spinning loop is blocked by the environment, not by the code: both profilers that would identify it are unavailable | E299 identified the failure as CPU-bound (worker at ~95% of a core for the whole budget, `utime` +380 ticks/4 s, non-terminating even at 300 s) and named the next step as profiling it. Both available tools are blocked: `perf record -p <pid>` produces no samples (`perf_event_paranoid=2` permits user-space recording only for processes you can attach to, and `perf.data` is never written), and `gdb -p <pid>` fails silently with `ptrace_scope=1` (the same restriction E195 recorded for live-attach; only launch-under-debugger works, which is what produced E240's result). So the two routes to a backtrace are both environment-blocked, and I am stopping here rather than adding a fourth call-site probe after E287/E289 showed how costly that is on this tree. What the session HAS established about this case, cumulatively: it is a pre-existing failure (E290), it stalls inside `app.submit` over a 3001-record memory window after seeding completes in 1.1 s (E294), its worker is actively running rather than waiting (E298), and it burns a full core without finishing even at 5x budget (E299). That is a coherent, four-measurement characterisation of a real product pathology with the specific loop unnamed and the tooling to name it unavailable. | `perf_event_paranoid=2`, no `perf.data` written; `ptrace_scope=1`, empty gdb output; E195/E287/E289/E294/E298/E299 |

| E301 | The spinning loop is NAMED by profile: per-row JSON re-serialization plus Cangjie generic type-info resolution, not a lock | E300 concluded both profilers were blocked; that was wrong for perf. `perf record -p <pid>` fails, but perf as the PARENT works (`ptrace_scope=1` allows a debugger to trace processes it spawns - the same distinction E240 found for gdb), after two fixes: perf drops `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`, so the target must be a wrapper script, and the direct `unittest_bin/agent_product` invocation needs the `pinned_cangjie cjpm test --skip-build` front end to select a case (a bare binary run recorded 1 sample). With the wrapper the run produced a **117 MB** profile. Flat symbols, `--percent-limit 1`: **9.45% `MapleRuntime::TypeInfoManager::GenericTiDescHashMap::GetGenericTiDesc`**, 2.60% `TypeInfo::GetMethodOuterTI`, 2.30% `GenericTiDesc::operator==`, 1.94% `wyhash`, 1.89%/1.67% `BaseObject::GetSize`, 1.83% `GetMTable`, 1.43% `memcpy_s`, and - the application-side signal - 1.43% `yjson.JsonNode.fields`, 1.38% `logical_json_payload.httpIntentDigest`, 1.35% `HashMap<String,JsonNode>.get`. So the dominant cost is Cangjie RUNTIME generic type-info lookup for the same generic types called in a tight loop, with the application frames being JSON field access and hashing. That matches the source: `memoryWindowContextNext` (`plugins/memory/src/native_window_context.cj:47-60`) loops `for(index in offset..headers.size)` and calls `stringifyUnifiedJson(empty)` PLUS four `header.field(...)` lookups PER ROW to compute each row's added size, so a 3001-row window does thousands of serialize+field-resolution iterations, each paying generic type-info resolution. The same file's next stage (`memoryWindowContextResult`, `:66-75`) then rebuilds `NativeMemoryJournal(state.records, ...)` - a per-fragment reconstruction over an ever-growing record list, i.e. quadratic - which is the more likely amplifier for a submit that never terminates at 3001 rows. I am recording the profile and these two candidate sites; I have NOT proven which dominates, and the quadratic reconstruction is a hypothesis from reading the loop structure. | `perf report -i /tmp/p5.data -g none --no-children --percent-limit 1`; `native_window_context.cj:47-60,66-75`; E300 corrected (perf IS usable as parent) |

| E302 | Checked E301's quadratic hypothesis and it does NOT hold as stated: the window is ONE fragment, so the cost is per-row work, not fragment accumulation | E301 suspected `NativeMemoryJournal(state.records, ...)` reconstruction over an ever-growing list. Arithmetic refutes the 'many fragments' version: the case's 3001 records are 'unrelated original record N' rows of roughly 150 bytes each, i.e. about **0.43 MiB total, so exactly ONE 8 MiB fragment** - `memoryWindowContextNext`'s loop breaks at `added > 8388608 - bytes` and one pass suffices. So there is no per-fragment accumulation to blame, and the profile's shape must come from per-row work: `memoryWindowContextNext` computes each row's added size by **`stringifyUnifiedJson` on a 4-field object plus four `header.field(...)` lookups**, and the profile's application frames (`yjson.JsonNode.fields` 1.43%, `HashMap<String,JsonNode>.get` 1.35%, `httpIntentDigest` 1.38%) are exactly those operations - while the dominant runtime frames (`GetGenericTiDesc` 9.45%) are the generic type-info resolution each such call pays in Cangjie. Is that enough to explain 60+ seconds for ~3000 rows? Not obviously: 3000 rows x (1 serialize + 4 field gets) is only ~15k operations, which should take milliseconds. So the size of the observed cost is NOT yet explained by this loop either, which means the profile's percentages (all 0.1-9% - no single dominant frame) point at something broader: the work is spread across many frames, consistent with `GetMethodOuterTI`/`GetMTable`/`GetSize` type-info machinery being hit from a large number of distinct call sites rather than one hot loop. I am recording this as an unresolved size question with the profile data attached, explicitly withdrawing E301's quadratic claim instead of leaving it standing. | arithmetic: 3000 x ~150 B = 0.43 MiB = 1 fragment; profile percentages from E301; `native_window_context.cj:47-60`; E301 partly withdrawn |

| E303 | The stall's call chain is now NAMED end to end, and the innermost frame is a HOST function, not the plugin | Captured an fp call-graph profile (`perf record -g` as parent, then `perf report -g graph,0.5,caller`, ~7 MB) and read the caller chain for the top 8.84% leaf. Reading it from the bottom up: `TestSuiteExecutor.runSingleCase` -> `InstalledNativeMemoryWindowApplicationTests.ActualApplicationFindsRecord3001...` -> **`ApplicationSession.submit`** -> `startOwnedCheckpointApplication113` -> `invokeOwnedCheckpointApplication113` -> `InProcessAgentClient.startOwnedCheckpointFailure113` -> `CoreRunService` -> **`AgentCore.start`** -> `startOwnedRunFrame159` -> **`continueRun`** -> **`prepareContextUpdate`** -> `executePreparationRead` -> `ToolPipeline.executePrepared` -> `executePreparedScope159` -> `ToolExecutor.executeObserved` -> **`HostContextReadExecutor.execute`** -> **`hostExecuteDocumentContextWindowRead`**. Two things this settles. (1) The stall is in the CONTEXT-READ path taken during `submit` (E294's localization is confirmed at function granularity, not just by stage marker): the submit drives `prepareContextUpdate`->`executePreparationRead`, i.e. the initial required-context read E253/E265 were about, and it ends in the Host's document-window read executor. (2) That innermost frame lives in `agent_host_runtime/src/plugin_document_context_window_preparation.cj:50`, a HOST file - so the file E301's application frames pointed at (`native_window_context.cj` in the memory plugin) is downstream of it, not the entry point. The chain also names `fn continueRun`/`prepareContextUpdate`, which per E232's masking map is exactly where a context-read failure becomes `context.query_invalid`. This is the most specific statement in the session about where the time goes; what it still does not say is WHY the window read is slow, and the profile's flat distribution (no frame > 9.45%) suggests the cost is a broad traversal rather than one loop. | perf `-g graph,0.5,caller` chain (verbatim above); `plugin_document_context_window_preparation.cj:50-76`; E294/E301/E302 |

| E304 | Two more candidate causes refuted by measurement, and the 'why slow' question is now narrowed but still open | Tested the two most plausible mechanics for a 60 s window read over 3001 rows. (1) Per-row plugin process spawning: sampled the worker's CHILD process count across the whole budget (`pgrep -P <worker>`) and it stayed **0** throughout (`max_children=0`) - the memory plugin is not spawned per row. (2) A per-row row loop in the Host reader: `HostArtifactStore.documentWindowPayloadReader` (`host_artifact_store.cj:352-...`) performs a SINGLE `SELECT ... FROM artifacts WHERE hash=?` and validates one row, with no loop over the window's rows; and `hostExecuteDocumentContextWindowRead` itself (`plugin_document_context_window_preparation.cj:86-110`) does one `reader.read()`. So the window read is not doing 3000 round trips or 3000 queries either. With E302 (no fragment accumulation) and E301 (no single frame above 9.45%) also refuting their candidates, the cost is a broadly distributed traversal that I have not identified, and I am recording the refutations rather than proposing a fifth unmeasured mechanism. The state of this line: the failure is real, pre-existing, CPU-bound, non-terminating at 5x budget, and its call chain is named (E303) down to `HostContextReadExecutor.execute`/`hostExecuteDocumentContextWindowRead`; what remains unidentified is the traversal itself. | `pgrep -P` child count 0 across 60 samples; `host_artifact_store.cj:352+` (single query); `plugin_document_context_window_preparation.cj:86-110`; E301/E302/E303 |

| E305 | MEASUREMENT ERROR and a safety issue I caused: my polling pattern matched ANOTHER session's worktree, and my earlier `pkill` was scoped unsafely | The GC/allocation measurement I had just run produced a thread named `cjdoc` with 137 CPU-seconds - a name that does not exist in the Cangjie runtime, which is what exposed the error: my pattern `pgrep -f "internalWorkerId=0"` is generic, and it matched `.../cjdoc-three-issues.6RzXh5/rebase130.gh/target/mock/release/unittest_bin/cjdoc --internalWorkerId=0 ...`, i.e. a DIFFERENT session's test worker in a different worktree, not mine. So that measurement is void. Worse, earlier in this session I ran `pkill -f "internalWorkerId"` while cleaning up - a pattern that would also have matched their workers. Checking now: their two runners (`std.testrunner` and `cjdoc`) show `etime` 8m19s, i.e. they have been alive LONGER than my cleanup, so I did not kill them this time; but the pattern was unsafe and that was luck rather than intent. Corrective actions taken: all my probes now match on the worktree path (`axyndra-pr49.R8V8Px`), my worktree's processes are cleaned to 0, and nothing of theirs was touched in this pass. Recording it because a measurement claimed in E305's sibling attempts and any earlier claim resting on that pattern are void, and because a future run must scope process selection by worktree path, not by a generic argument name that other sessions also use. | their `etime` 08:19 for both runners (older than my cleanup); my pattern matched `cjdoc-three-issues`; E304's sibling GC measurement withdrawn |

| E306 | Corrected GC/allocation measurement with a worktree-scoped pattern: GC is ~11% of the cost, and exactly ONE child process exists | Redid E305's void measurement matching on the worktree path. Threads observed (all genuine Cangjie runtime names, no foreign `cjdoc`): `agent_product` (main), `gc-pool-t1`, `gc-main-thread`, `gc-helper`, `schmon`, and four `schd-worker`s. CPU ticks across the budget: the busiest `schd-worker` went 63 -> **4749** (~47 s of CPU at 100 Hz), `gc-main-thread` 7 -> **399** and `gc-pool-t1` 4 -> **204**, i.e. the two GC threads together account for ~603 ticks against ~4686 for the working thread - **GC is roughly 11% of the cost**, not the dominant term. Also `MAXKIDS=1`: the worker has exactly ONE child throughout, consistent with E304's finding that the memory plugin is spawned once for the invocation rather than per row (E304's own `internalWorkerId=0` sampling was itself unsafe by the same pattern as E305, so its conclusion is re-established here rather than merely repeated). So allocation pressure is real but secondary; the bulk of the ~47 CPU-seconds is in the worker thread executing the window read, which is where E303's chain points, and the traversal's identity remains the open question. | scoped sample: schd-worker 63->4749, gc-main 7->399, gc-pool-t1 4->204, `MAXKIDS=1`; E301/E303/E304/E305 |

| E307 | The measured cost profile points at framework overhead, not at the window algorithm: ~59% runtime/std, 17% application, 7% SQLite, 5% SHA-256 | Recorded a fresh fp profile and read it three ways. Top symbols (unchanged in shape from E301): 9.70% `GenericTiDescHashMap::GetGenericTiDesc`, 2.79% `GetMethodOuterTI`, 2.64% `GenericTiDesc::operator==`, 2.08%+1.72% `BaseObject::GetSize` (GC), 1.77% `GetMTable`, 1.50% `memcpy_s`, 1.38% `yjson.JsonNode.fields`, 1.34% `Barrier::WriteGeneric`, 1.25% `HashMap<String,JsonNode>.get`, 1.16% `httpIntentDigest`, 1.01% `WCollector::TraceRefField`, 0.99% `GenericTiDesc::computeHash`. By DSO the split is decisive: **libcangjie-runtime 47.9% + std-core 9.1% + std-collection 2.1% = ~59% runtime/std**, agent_product **17.0%**, libsqlite3 **7.0%**, libcrypto **5.3%** (the SHA-256 behind `httpIntentDigest`), libc+boundscheck ~9.6%. My window-symbol grep found essentially nothing (`requirePluginDocumentContextWindowReadScope` at 0.06%), i.e. no single window function is hot. Read together with E306 (GC ~11%, worker thread ~47 CPU-s) this says the time is dominated by Cangjie generic type-info resolution and related runtime machinery being invoked a very large number of times, with the application frames being JSON field access, HashMap lookup and repeated SHA-256 - i.e. a per-item cost that is many times what these operations should cost in a compiled language. That is a characterisation, not a diagnosis: it still does not name the code path that issues the enormous number of small typed operations. The direction it does suggest, for whoever continues: the generic-type-info and barrier costs (Collection/GenericInstance, `WriteGeneric`, `GetGenericTiDesc`) are a known-heavy Cangjie pattern, so look for a loop that touches generic collections per item rather than for one expensive algorithm. | `perf report` top symbols and DSO split above; E301/E306 |

| E308 | Concrete, testable hypothesis for the cost: per-event full JSON parse plus SHA-256 in `memoryDocumentHistory`, called on the 3001/3000-row window | E307's profile said the cost is many small typed operations, not one algorithm. Reading the memory plugin's history reconstruction finds exactly that shape: `memoryDocumentHistory` (`plugins/memory/src/native_document_tools.cj:62-86`) loops over the journal's `rows` AND `events` and, per item, does `httpIntentDigest(...)` (a SHA-256) and - for every event - `parseUnifiedJson(raw, maxBytes: 8388608, maxDepth: 32)` followed by `memoryV3DecodeEvent`. For the case under test that is 3001 rows + 3000 events, i.e. ~6000 SHA-256 digests and 3000 full JSON parses of the original payload, each through `JsonNode.parse` with `JsonReadOptions` (`yjson_support/src/json_support.cj:189-205`) - a constructor-heavy path that matches the profile's generic type-info/`GetGenericTiDesc` dominance and the 1.16% `httpIntentDigest` application frame. Arithmetic: at ~0.2 ms per parse the total is 0.6 s (too fast to explain 60 s) and at ~20 ms it is exactly 60 s, so the hypothesis is quantitatively plausible but only if each parse/decode is expensive in Cangjie - which is precisely what the runtime-heavy profile suggests. The test is cheap and discriminating: time `parseUnifiedJson` + `memoryV3DecodeEvent` on this exact record shape in a loop, and compare against the 60 s budget with 6000 iterations. I have NOT run that test, so this is recorded as the leading hypothesis with its own test, not as a finding. | `native_document_tools.cj:62-86`; `json_support.cj:189-205`; the case's 3001 rows + 3000 events; E301/E306/E307 |

| E309 | E305's safety check is confirmed by the other session's continued activity, and this session's state is clean | Followed up on E305 rather than leaving the concern open. The `cjdoc-three-issues` session is demonstrably ALIVE and working: its live processes include a performance-gate run and a code-generation run in ITS OWN worktree (the perf-gate script path belongs to that worktree, not to this repository) plus its browser tunnel helper, while its two `unittest_bin` runners are gone. That is consistent with E305's evidence (their runners had `etime` 8m19s, older than my cleanup) and with the natural reading that their test run FINISHED on its own rather than being killed by my `pkill`. Session state re-verified after all of this turn's work: ledger at E309, six repo gates green (`docs_gate`, `architecture_gate`, `dependency_pin_gate`, `plugin_boundary_gate`, `check_library_boundaries`, `tracked_artifact_gate`), App hash `a575949a...` unchanged, zero probe markers in the tree, zero processes in my worktree, zero private-path patterns in the diff, and no perf data left in tmpfs. | their live processes (`perf_gate.py`, `main generate`) vs zero `unittest_bin`; E305 |

| E310 | E308's hypothesis is REFUTED by direct measurement: the per-event JSON parse costs 8 ms, not 60 s | Built the discriminating test E308 called for rather than leaving it as a hypothesis: a standalone Cangjie program parsing one representative journal-event payload with the EXACT production options (`JsonNode.parse`, `JsonDuplicateKeyPolicy.Reject`, `maxDepth: 32`, 8 MiB string/input/buffer bounds) in a 3000-iteration loop, warmed first. Result, two runs: **`parsed 3000 events in 8 ms`, `per-parse us=2`**. So the whole parse workload of this case is ~8 milliseconds - four orders of magnitude below the 60 s budget - and `httpIntentDigest` over 64-char hex is cheaper still (SHA-256 of a few dozen bytes). E308's quantitative precondition ('only if each parse/decode is expensive in Cangjie') is therefore FALSE, and the per-item-parse explanation for the stall is withdrawn. That is the fifth candidate eliminated by measurement (after fragment accumulation, single hot frame, per-row process spawn, per-row SQL loop, and GC dominance), which is itself informative: what remains must be in the traversal or in a path the profile's flat distribution hides, not in the per-row JSON work the source made look expensive. Method note: the benchmark needed the repo's `yjson` at tag 0.1.0 (already in `~/.cjpm/git/yjson`) and the SDK's dynamic stdx on `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`; it is a throwaway under `$HOME/.cache/agent-tmp` and produces no repo changes. | benchmark output `parsed 3000 events in 8 ms / per-parse us=2` (two runs); `yjson_support/src/json_support.cj:189-205`; E308 withdrawn |

| E313 | A concrete quadratic candidate found in the window path's validation, with its arithmetic | After five refutations the search moved to the store/business layer rather than the JSON layer, and one pattern stands out with a matching shape. `memories.validateContextRecords(hits)` is called on the context-read path (`native_memory_tools.cj:635`, and again at `native_tool_policy.cj:46`), and its body (`native_journal_business.cj:493-501`) loops over EVERY record calling `memoryV3Find(current, record.id)` - and `memoryV3Find` (`:622-625`) is a LINEAR scan (`for (record in records where record.id == id)`). With `current` derived by `loadCurrent()` -> `loadHistory()` -> `currentRecords(history)` (`:514-517`), the worst case for a 3001-record window is ~3001 x 3001 = **9.0 million** id comparisons, each allocating a `MemoryV3Record`-comparing closure over a String key - and the profile's dominant application frames are exactly String-hash/`JsonNode.fields` work with generic type-info resolution (E301/E307). This is the FIRST candidate whose cost scales the way the symptom requires (it grows with the window, which is why the case is named for record 3001 and why 60 s and 300 s both fail), whereas the per-event parse E310 measured was flat and fast. It is NOT proven: 9 M comparisons should still be well under a second in a compiled language unless each comparison is very expensive, and I have not measured it - so this is recorded as the leading hypothesis with its arithmetic, to be confirmed by the same cheap benchmark method E310 used (time `validateContextRecords` over a synthetic 3001-record set) or by reducing the window and observing the scaling. | `native_journal_business.cj:493-501,514-517,622-625`; `native_memory_tools.cj:635`; `native_tool_policy.cj:46`; E301/E307/E310 |

| E314 | Scaling attempt abandoned mid-flight, and the better test for E313 is standing, not doing | Tried to measure E313 the cheapest way - shrink the case's window from 3001 to 301 rows and compare runtime, which would separate an O(n) traversal from an O(n^2) validation in one run. Two problems, both recorded rather than worked around. (1) My edit produced `38762929 ns` with `PASSED: 0, ERROR: 1` - a fixture ERROR, not the timeout, because I ran it WITHOUT the gate fixture environment (the E222/E234/E292 trap for the fourth time this session), so that number is not a valid measurement of anything. (2) Editing the case to change its window means editing a tracked test file, and while I reverted it immediately and verified it byte-clean against HEAD, that is the wrong instrument for a measurement: a throwaway benchmark like E310's (which parses in 8 ms) is cheaper and carries no repo risk. So E313 remains an unmeasured hypothesis with its arithmetic, and this turn adds only the method guidance: use a standalone benchmark, not a modified case, and always export the fixture environment before any case run. Restored state verified: `agent_product/src/plugin_memory_window_application_test.cj` is clean vs HEAD with its 3001-row bound intact; no other file touched. | `/tmp/scale301.log` (`38762929 ns`, `ERROR: 1` = missing fixture env); `git diff` clean after revert; E292/E310/E313 |

| E315 | E313's candidate confirmed to be ON the failing path, so the remaining question is purely whether it is expensive enough | Verified the call chain from the window result handler rather than assuming: `memoryWindowContextResult` (`plugins/memory/src/native_window_context.cj:66-75`) builds `NativeMemoryTools(journal, ...)` where `journal = NativeMemoryJournal(state.records, ...)` - i.e. ALL records of the window become the plugin's journal - and then calls `runtime.contextFor(...)`. That function (`native_memory_tools.cj:598-640`) ends by calling `memories.validateContextRecords(hits.toArray().map { hit => hit.memory })` (`:635`), which is exactly the loop that runs a linear `memoryV3Find` per record (E313). So the quadratic shape is reached from the failing path with the full window as input, and the only open question is the constant factor: 9.0 M comparisons could be single-digit milliseconds or tens of seconds depending on how each comparison and each `MemoryV3Record` closure allocation behaves in Cangjie. That is precisely what E310's benchmark method answers in one run, and it is now the single next step for this line - the hypothesis, its arithmetic, its call path and its test method are all settled. | `native_window_context.cj:66-75`; `native_memory_tools.cj:598-640`; `native_journal_business.cj:493-501,622-625`; E310/E313/E314 |

| E316 | E313/E315 partially REFUTED by closer reading: the loop iterates the bounded HITS, not all 3001 records, and the only per-call full-set cost is a bounded history load | Read `validateContextRecords`'s argument rather than its name: it is called with `hits.toArray().map { hit => hit.memory }` (`native_memory_tools.cj:635`), so the outer loop runs once per SEARCH HIT, not once per window record. Only `memoryV3Find(current, record.id)` scans the full set (`current` = `loadCurrent()` = all 3001), so the cost is `hits x 3001` id comparisons, not `3001 x 3001` as E313 stated - and hit counts are bounded by the memory context window (`maxBytes`), typically a handful. Arithmetic at 3001 scan steps per hit is microseconds, and E310 already measured a full 3000-event history load at 8 ms, so even the `loadCurrent()` term is bounded at ~8 ms per call. Therefore this path is ~milliseconds per call, and explaining 60 s would require it to be invoked tens of thousands of times - which nothing I have found suggests. So E313's '9.0 M comparisons' figure is WRONG (it assumed the outer loop covered all records) and its candidate is now the SIXTH refuted rather than the leading one. I am recording this rather than letting the more dramatic number stand, and noting the general lesson for this investigation: the profile says the cost is spread thinly across enormous numbers of operations, so the culprit is almost certainly a call count, not a per-call algorithm - and I have not found the call site that runs on the order of 10^4-10^5 times. | `native_memory_tools.cj:635` (hits, not records); `native_journal_business.cj:493-503,514-517`; E310's 8 ms history-load measurement; E313/E315 partly withdrawn |

| E317 | Continuation summary of the current blocker and what would finish it | The unit gate's remaining blocker is now fully characterised but unnamed at the call-count level. Established by measurement this session: (1) it is in THIS PR's scope - the case and the window path both exist at the base commit `e53b9f65` (E312); (2) it stalls in the context-read path during `submit`, chain named to `HostContextReadExecutor.execute`/`hostExecuteDocumentContextWindowRead` (E294/E303); (3) it is CPU-bound, ~95% of a core, non-terminating at 5x the gate's budget (E299); (4) GC is only ~11% and the DSO split is 59% Cangjie runtime/std, 17% application, 7% SQLite, 5% crypto (E306/E307); (5) SIX candidate mechanisms are refuted by measurement - fragment accumulation (E302), a single hot frame (E301), per-row process spawn and per-row SQL loop (E304), GC dominance (E306), per-event JSON parse (E310, benchmarked at 8 ms for 3000 parses), and the quadratic `validateContextRecords` scan (E316, its loop is over hits, not all records). What remains is stated as a property rather than a guess: because no frame exceeds ~10% and each identified operation is milliseconds, the culprit must be a CALL COUNT on the order of 10^4-10^5, not a per-call algorithm. The concrete next probes are therefore (a) count invocations of `contextFor`/`validateContextRecords`/`readWholeFrame` during one run, or (b) profile with call-graph aggregation for counts rather than percentages. Method constraints for whoever continues: sample processes by WORKTREE path (E305 - a generic `internalWorkerId` pattern matched another session's worktree); always export the gate fixture environment before running any case (E292/E314, four occurrences); use standalone benchmarks for cost questions (E310) rather than editing tracked tests (E314); and verify a build before trusting any probe value (E287/E289). | E292/E294/E299/E301-E307/E310-E316 |

| E318 | The call-count probe was INVALID by design, not a finding - and the reason is itself informative | E317's next step was to count invocations. I implemented it inside the memory plugin's context provider (increment a counter, write the value to a file) and rebuilt the fixture with `prepare_memory_plugin.py`, exporting it as `AXYNDRA_MEMORY_PLUGIN_PACKAGE` - which the fixture DOES honour (`plugin_memory_policy_test.cj:43` reads exactly that variable, so the instrumented package was the one installed). The probe file was never created, twice (once writing every 200 calls, once every call). That is NOT evidence that the provider never runs: the plugin executes as a SEPARATE, SANDBOXED process, and my `try/catch` around the file write swallowed whatever the sandbox returned - so absence of the file is uninformative. Two lessons, both recorded rather than worked around: (a) probes that write from inside a plugin must go through a channel the sandbox permits (the plugin's stdout protocol, or the host's own logging), not to `/tmp`; and (b) a caught-and-ignored exception in a probe destroys the very signal the probe exists to produce, which is how a silently-failing write nearly became a false negative finding. Reverted: `native_context_provider.cj` is byte-identical to its backup and clean vs HEAD, and both fixture builds plus the probe file were removed. The call-count question E317 posed therefore remains UNANSWERED, with this turn contributing the method constraint for answering it. | `plugin_memory_policy_test.cj:43`; the two empty probe runs; `git diff` clean after revert |

| E319 | Profile refined: type-info resolution 17.8%, GC/marking 8.0%, application JSON/hash 6.5% - the top cost is Cangjie GENERIC TYPE METADATA, not application logic | Third independent profile, aggregated by family with `--percent-limit 0.05`: type-info machinery (`GetGenericTiDesc` 8.95 + `GenericTiDesc::operator==` 2.49 + `GetMethodOuterTI` 2.41 + `GetMTable` 1.46 + `GetOrCreateTypeInfo` 1.37 + `computeHash` 1.14) = **17.8%**; GC/marking (`BaseObject::GetSize` 3.95 + `VisitLiveObjectsUntilFalse` 1.64 + `TraceRefField` 1.35 + `MarkBits` 1.05) = **8.0%**; application JSON/hash iteration (`yjson.JsonNode.fields` 1.74 + `httpIntentDigest` 1.34 + `HashMap<String,JsonNode>.get` 1.26 + `ArrayIterator.next` 1.24 + `String ==` 0.87) = **6.5%**; runtime write/generic barriers ~3.9%. So the single largest family is resolving GENERIC TYPE METADATA - the cost Cangjie pays for calling generic-instantiated code - which is consistent with, and sharpens, E317's conclusion: an enormous NUMBER of generic calls, not an expensive algorithm. Practically this means the fix direction is either reducing call count in the window path or restructuring the hot data (e.g. wider typed paths instead of per-item generic Collection/JsonNode access), and it means a profile-guided micro-optimisation of any single function will not help. Recording as the refined characterisation; the specific call site that issues those calls is still not identified, and E318's channel constraint is what blocks counting them from inside the plugin. | `perf report -sort symbol --percent-limit 0.05` families above; E301/E307/E317 |

| E335 | Two more structural exclusions on the oversized-demand search, so the next attempt starts from a narrower set | Continuing E333/E334's narrowing. To demand 105 MiB in one growth, such a call needs an input of roughly 6.6 MiB at 16x, or 13 MiB at 8x - both ABOVE the 4.25 MiB content-payload profile and the 16 MiB message bound only in the 8x case. So the source is either a 16x call with an input larger than the content profile, or a call whose input is a SUM (e.g. `retained + invoice`, `retainedPeak + ...`) where the growth is the difference from an already-large basis. Both shapes exist in the list (e.g. `ensureRetainedPeak(retained + invoice)`, `ensureRetainedPeak(retainedPeak+1048576+indexBytes*8+...)`), and distinguishing them needs the E322-style counter on the SPECIFIC caller rather than a tree-wide grep. That is the third structural exclusion on this search (after E333's `ensureDecoded` and E334's invoice-formula), and it leaves the demand in a caller that (a) grows directly, (b) is not in the compaction plugin, (c) has per-item factors of 16x or a summed basis, and (d) has NO 128 MiB guard of its own - which is a small, enumerable set: re-reading E335's 30 forms, the unguarded large-factor ones with byte-scaled inputs are `1048576 + file.text.size * 16 + nodes180 * 1024` and `1048576 + measured.bytes * 16 + measured.nodes * 512`. | E355 | The shared-custody route now has a named mechanism, which is the most specific lead in this line | E354 identified that `grow` is also reached without any caller-supplied large constant. Reading the two shared paths gives the exact formula. (a) `deriveRetainedLease` (`logical_json_payload.cj:160-172`): when a reservation has no shared ledger yet, it computes `wanted = max(cost, declared + 1024)` and grows by `wanted - cost` - so the growth is driven by the reservation's own `declared` value, which `ensureRetainedPeak` sets as `declared = max(declared, minimum)` (`:196`). A reservation whose `declared` had previously been raised to a large figure will therefore demand that figure again on the next derive. (b) `ensure` (`:236-250`): `growth = minimum - previous`, then `wanted = sum + growth` and `grow(wanted - cost)` - again driven by the caller's `minimum` against a running `sum`. So a 105.2 MiB growth requires some reservation whose `declared` (or a ledger `sum`) has reached ~105 MiB, and the demand then appears on a LATER operation than whichever call first set it. That reframes the search in a way that explains nine misses: the offending value may be SET by one call and DEMANDED by a different, later one - so probing the setting sites (which I did) would show small values while the demand appears elsewhere. The instrument that fits this shape is a per-reservation high-water record inside `LogicalJsonOwnerBudget` (which reservation, and what its `declared`/`sum` reached), not a per-call-site probe. | `logical_json_payload.cj:160-172,196,236-250`; E354 |

| E356 | BREAKTHROUGH: the demand is fully traced - a DERIVED lease's first growth inside the shared ledger, and the arithmetic closes exactly | E355 predicted the value would be set by one call and demanded by a later one through the shared-custody route, and the per-reservation high-water instrument (which E355 identified as the right tool) caught it on the first run: **`LEDGER sum=13917328 growth=115957248 cost=19579360 grow=110295216`**. Every number reconciles with E347's independent measurement: `wanted = sum + growth = 13917328 + 115957248 = 129,874,576`, and `grow = wanted - cost = 129,874,576 - 19,579,360 = 110,295,216`, which is EXACTLY the `grow=110295216` E347 captured from inside the pool. So the mechanism is: a DERIVED lease was created against a reservation whose `cost` was 19,579,360, the caller then requested `ensureRetainedPeak` with a `minimum` **115,957,248 (110.6 MiB) higher than that lease's previous value**, and because the ledger's `sum` (13,917,328) plus that growth exceeded the reservation's `cost`, the ledger asked the bank to grow by 110.3 MiB - which the 128 MiB bank (holding 36,372,960) refused. Two things follow. (a) This is NOT a caller passing a 105 MiB constant; it is ONE lease jumping ~110.6 MiB in a single `ensure` call, which is why ten call-site probes all showed small values - the growth is a DELTA computed inside the ledger from the caller's requested `minimum` minus that lease's prior value. (b) The precise question for the caller is now unambiguous: who calls `ensureRetainedPeak(~115,957,248)` (or 6.85 MiB at 16x, 13.70 MiB at 8x, per the factor arithmetic) on a derived lease? That is a single, narrow query against the tree rather than a search. Cross-checked against E356: the delta `116088320 - 131072 = 115,957,248` is EXACTLY the `growth=115957248` the ledger reported, so this is the same event seen from the other side, not a coincidence. So the failing operation is a `derived lease` (id 969) whose first `ensure` request is `minimum = 116,088,320` while its previous value was 128 KiB - one jump of 110.7 MiB. Factoring `116,088,320` against the known shapes: `- 1048576` gives `115,039,744`, which `/16 = 7,189,984` - i.e. the formula is the familiar `1048576 + bytes*16 (+ nodes*512)` with `bytes` around **6.86 MiB** (6.83 MiB at 1024 nodes, 6.73 MiB at 4096). | E376 | TOOLING DISCIPLINE: python text-mode I/O silently corrupts this tree, and one probe file had to be restored from the git object | Two hard facts for anyone probing this repo. | E381 | gdb breakpoint mechanics now fully explained: the case runs in a fork+EXEC'd worker, so a parent breakpoint can never fire, and the fix is a concrete next step | With all four E365 preconditions applied and `set follow-fork-mode child`, the log states the mechanism outright: `Attaching after Thread ... fork to child process <pid>` followed by `process <pid> is executing new program: <...>/agent_product/target/release/unittest_bin/agent_product`. The unit runner therefore does not merely fork - it **fork+execs a fresh image of the same binary** to run the case, which is why the `grow` breakpoint set in the parent resolves (`Breakpoint 1 at 0x3a609f8`) but never fires, and why `follow-exec-mode new` + `catch exec` then tripped the runner's own guard (E367: `Expected execution permission from the Main process`) instead of yielding frames: the runner handshakes with its worker and rejects a debugger that intercepts the exec. The precise fix is a gdb script that (a) does NOT stop the exec but re-arms the `grow` breakpoint once the new image is loaded, e.g. `catch exec` with a handler that runs `break _CN20logical_json_payload22LogicalJsonOwnerBudget4growHl` and `continue` while leaving the runner's handshake uninterrupted, or equivalently `set stop-on-solib-events` / a `commands` block on the exec catchpoint; this is the single gdb artifact I have not produced correctly (my earlier attempt used `silent` outside a `commands` block, which gdb rejected with `Undefined command: "silent"`). () from /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2`, then argument-vector frames) because it was armed before the post-exec image's symbols were mapped. (b) `follow-exec-mode new` (without `detach-on-fork off`): gdb followed the exec chain into the app image and then straight out of it, reporting `executing new program` for `.../unittest_bin/agent_product`, then `/usr/bin/timeout`, then `/usr/bin/bwrap`, then `/usr/bin/prlimit`. That is the decisive fact: the unit worker fork+execs the app image and that image in turn execs the plugin-sanitising helpers, so a debugger told to follow exec chases the sandbox launch chain instead of the case. This also independently corroborates E318 (the plugin process really does run under `bwrap`) and shows why E367's guard fired. The remaining fix options are therefore narrow and known: (i) `catch exec` with a handler that waits for the *second* exec of the app path before arming, or (ii) `set follow-exec-mode same` combined with `set scheduler-locking`, or (iii) break on a symbol that exists only after the sandbox launch. I am stopping the gdb line here having reached the tenth-eleventh invocation, with the mechanism fully documented; the caller remains unnamed. | `#0 0x...7fdf7e0 in ?? So the three outcomes are exhaustive: (i) breakpoint in the parent never fires (the case runs in a fork+exec child), (ii) following the exec misses the app image or resolves against `ld-linux`/`/usr/bin/true` (the exec tree fans out through the sandbox launch), (iii) stopping the exec deadlocks the runner's handshake. There is no gdb configuration that both follows the app image and leaves the handshake intact, because gdb and the runner disagree about who controls the forked child. Consequence for the task: the caller of the ~7 MiB decoded-payload request cannot be named by gdb in this harness, and the only remaining routes are a positive probe placed inside the sandboxed plugin path or a change to the runner - both out of scope for a PR that must not alter the code under test. App215 uses the installed daily SDK directly: `cjpm build -i -j4 -m agent_app --target-dir <evidence>/core-development-build200/target`.
The current frozen Native library is supplied unchanged through `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`.

`python3 scripts/product_unit_gate.py --output-dir <evidence>/unit-tests`.

success does not mean those cases have executed.

## Approved verification-boundary decisions

The user explicitly authorized these decisions during this continuation:

- Isolate every Host test case in a fresh process. Keep the complete inventory,
  original assertions, inputs, Native limits, whole-module timeout and cleanup.
  This resolves the incompatible same-process lifetimes documented in E37;
  physical process exit is not a fabricated DATA-consumption acknowledgement.
- Rebuild unmodified Native sources only in task-owned isolated caches for the
  original full unit and Minimal gates. Native C and the installed SDK remain
  frozen; only the explicitly listed Native exceptions below are authorized.
- Set async.enabled=false only for the Workflow fixture that asserts completed
  immediately after its parent Run. Keep its original assertions, business input,
  timeouts and cleanup. Product defaults and background acceptance are unchanged.
- Add explicit get_subagents includeTerminal:true for all canonical session
  snapshots. Absent/false remains active-only; reject non-booleans. Only the
  Workflow query changes; its completed-child assertions remain unchanged.
- Replace the old two-service selection maximum with the existing closed twelve
  canonical names and duplicate rejection. Preserve child/process exclusivity,
  Native limits and timeouts. Migrate only the superseded maximum assertion.
- Correct only the Workflow Native Task Schema declaration to expose its existing
  Single/Batch parser, then rebuild in isolation. Keep all seven original Peer
  inputs, execution algorithms, Native C, SDK, budgets and deadlines unchanged.
- Correct only the Workflow test oracle to reconstruct duplicate keys through
  the existing ordered put API. Preserve its original duplicate inputs and
  assertions; rerun the complete unit and protocol gates.
- Correct only the real workflowYieldedOutput111 output assembler to use the
  existing ordered put API for duplicate section labels. The full rerun moved
  its remaining error from the corrected test oracle to this actual Task helper.
  Preserve original overwrite order, inputs, assertions and every other Native
  algorithm, Native C, SDK, budget and deadline; rerun complete unit/protocol gates.

Twelve positive unregisterTool preconditions for retired Bash builtins were
removed across six existing test files; no test case, business assertion, grant,
input, timeout or cleanup was removed. Actual Shell/Workspace callers already
use installed canonical contributions. Restoring a fake builtin is not allowed.

this migration is not behavioral acceptance evidence.

## Current failure boundaries and next verification

**Current state after the E90-E105 work (supersedes the older notes below where they conflict).**

- Three real defects were found and fixed in production/test code, each with
  before/after evidence: the frozen plan environment contradiction (E90), the
  masked registry-capacity classification (E95), and the rewind checkpoint that
  hid its own Turn intent (E98). `tool_runtime` 55/55, `agent_domain` 174/174 and
  `agent_runtime` 29/29 pass, and the previously blocked Core rewind consumer and
  the plan-review pressure consumer both pass now.
- The complete workspace gate builds all sixteen plugin fixtures from source and
  passes **26 of 27 packages** (E93, re-confirmed in E97); an isolated rebuild of
  the unmodified Native sources reproduces the frozen artifacts byte-for-byte
  (E94). `agent_product` is the only failing package.
- The exact size of `agent_product`'s remaining failure set is **not**
  established. The gate stops at the first failing case, and the two ad-hoc
  sweeps attempted in this session were invalid instruments (E101), so no
  trustworthy per-case count exists yet.
- The single blocking implementation item is the shared Host bank lifetime
  (E91, E103). A 16 MiB floor per `acquire` against a 128 MiB aggregate allows at
  most eight owners, each fixture mount retains roughly 33.9 MB past its closure,
  and session slot reservations charge the same floor. The consumers blocked by
  it are the private-field loop representatives, the Core checkpoint/history
  family (which reports the designed `application.owned_failure_pending`
  terminal, E102/E104) and the maintenance-failure bank-pressure assertion.
- The cheapest candidate fix was falsified: removing the unconditional
  success-path `retainLegacy159()` at `javascript_plugin_host.cj:599` and
  rebuilding leaves the loop failure identical (E105). The remaining work needs
  the counted private-claim ownership design, not a one-line retention change.
- All A0-A7 and DoD-1..DoD-6 acceptance requirements remain open, and
  whole-workspace acceptance cannot be claimed while `agent_product` fails.

- A second independent boundary was measured this session: the release
  contract stage. `release_gate.sh` stops at the first failing contract, and
  the draft commit had taken that whole stage out of service - `goal_contract`,
  `product_thread_runtime_contract`, `sqlite_run_repository_contract`,
  `memory_contract`, `product_prompt_contract` and `sandbox_contract` were all
  green in the base release list. After the repairs recorded as E126-E137 the
  layer measures 50 of 51 packages building (`contract-audit3.log`), so six
  previously-broken release contracts are restored and only `goal_contract`
  remains; `goal_contract` was never edited by the draft, so its break is a
  regression against the deleted goal runtime rather than an expected gap.
- `product_prompt_contract` builds but still cannot run to completion: its
  `retain` call reaches the Memory package contribution through an unprepared
  Direct invocation, which the runtime rejects by design (E139). Its goal
  assertions therefore have no execution evidence yet.

- The complete working-source architecture gate now passes (E77), and its real
  non-Core Model-caller negative is rejected. It does not prove runtime authority
  or physical worker return. Both original async lifecycle cases fail in E74;
  fresh original RPC compilation and SQL/receipt behavior remain required with
  unchanged complete inventory. No worker rename or Host Peer business fallback
  was introduced to satisfy the removed incidental predicates.
- Publication classification now covers all 39 workspace members, including
  the three omitted internal packages. This source audit does not replace
  source-package staging or final Minimal/Coding/Full artifact acceptance.
- E63 passes the original Workflow 31 units and 169 protocols with actual
  frozen-fourteen-library loader records. Three public synchronous Workflow

  async behavior is unchanged. The original seven Product Peer checks remain

- Stage223 executes the complete real PTY suite: nine pass; actual MCP HTTP

## Compatibility and recovery

This is the breaking plugin-first cutover, not a dual runtime. Recognized legacy
lock rows upgrade only on an explicit writer mutation. Unsupported versions,
unknown fields and unsupported typed facts must fail before publication and
retain the original bytes. SQLite future versions must be rejected before WAL
configuration or writes. Plugin rollback restores content, not revoked grants;
database, plugin and binary rollback are distinct operations. Historical missing
implementations must block execution while preserving supported inspection; they
must not be substituted with a newer plugin. Final migration/backup/upgrade
results remain required before readiness.
The managed Registry static-reason variant is now `PluginRegistryStaticReason.NoStaticReason`;
all callers must migrate from the removed ambiguous `None` variant. No alias or
legacy constructor/runtime fallback is retained.

Unsafe writer ancestors retain the existing `plugin.lock_write_failed` error
classification for Native ENOTDIR/ELOOP rejection. Read-only fence errors keep
their existing classification. The fence still rejects before external plugin
directories are created; no Native path guard or installed SDK is changed (E68).

No checklist is marked complete by this record. Use `Refs #45`, not a closing
reference, while applicable requirements remain unaccepted.
